assumptions.

Readers are advised to use caution when interpreting forward-looking statements relating to the Group’s business strategy, plans, objectives and financial positions 
as these statements rely on assumptions and hypotheses, which inherently represent an accuracy risk. Actual results, performance and events may differ from 
those described in such statements due to unexpected changes in the economic, political, sanitary, industry, interest rate and currency market conditions as well as 
developments in relation to applicable laws and regulations. The Group does not undertake to update any forward-looking statement contained herein or that may be 
made from time to time by the organisation or on its behalf.

## The  concept

## Our Shared Heritage, Our Shared Future

The ocean is central to who we are and to the future of our region. It shapes our identity, supports our economies and our 
communities, and plays an essential role in our everyday lives.

For generations, it has inspired artists who have captured our relationship with the sea, reflecting our cultural heritage and 
enriching the artistic identity of our islands.

As MCB strengthens its commitment to a sustainable blue economy, we have placed the ocean at the heart of this year’s 
Sustainability Report. Through a selection of artworks by artists, past and present, we celebrate this shared heritage and its 
importance for future generations.

## About this Report

This report provides an overview of our corporate sustainability programme and 
tells you more about the actions we took during the year to deliver on our purpose, 
Success Beyond Numbers.

## Our reporting scope and boundary

We have also communicated material events after this 
date and until the report’s approval. Initiatives in this 
report largely cover the Bank’s non-financial performance, 
although some key performance indicators relate to the 
Group’s operations. We differentiate this clearly throughout 
the report. Data relating to financial, economic, social and 
environmental indicators remains comparable to and 
consistent with our 2025 Sustainability Report, except where 
restatements or methodology changes are noted.

This report covers the sustainability performance of MCB 
Group Limited (the Group), a prominent regional financial 
services player consisting of local and overseas banking 
subsidiaries and associates. The Group’s main subsidiary is 
Mauritius Commercial Bank Ltd (MCB Ltd, MCB or the Bank), 
the leading bank in Mauritius. 
 
This report covers the period 1 July 2025 to 30 June 2026 
(FY26, aligned with the Group’s financial reporting period).

## Our reporting suite

We produce a suite of annual publications tailored to meet our stakeholders’ specific information requirements.

Our Sustainability Report complements our Annual Report 
and describes the Group’s direct and indirect impacts on 
society, the environment and the economy alongside the 
role we play in shaping resilient economies, supporting 
communities, protecting the environment and enabling 
more inclusive growth. It also sets out our progress in 
identifying and assessing sustainability-related risks and 
opportunities as we work towards alignment with the IFRS 
Sustainability Disclosure Standards.

Our Annual Report provides a holistic account of how 
MCB Group creates, delivers and preserves value for 
its stakeholders over the short, medium and long term. 
This includes providing insight into the Group’s strategy, 
governance, financial performance, key risks (including 
climate and other sustainability risks) and future prospects.

## Reporting frameworks and standards

We developed this report with reference to the Global Reporting Initiative’s (GRI) Sustainability Reporting Standards. We 
also consider the Sustainability Accounting Standards Board (SASB) Standards and disclose selected metrics relevant to our 
operations. In addition, we continue to strengthen our alignment with evolving sustainability reporting standards, including the 
IFRS Sustainability Disclosure Standards (IFRS S1 and S2).

Read more about our IFRS S1 and S2 implementation roadmap on page 43. Our IFRS S1 and S2 Content Index, GRI Content Index and SASB Content Index are 
available in the Appendices, from page 206.

## Materiality

In FY24, MCB Ltd undertook a materiality assessment to identify the sustainability-related impacts, risks and opportunities 
most relevant to the Bank and its stakeholders. The assessment was informed by the GRI Standards (GRI 3: Material Topics 
2021), as well as the evolving disclosure expectations of IFRS S1 and S2 and the European Sustainability Reporting Standards. 
During FY26, we made progress translating our identified sustainability impacts into related risks and opportunities, 
developed a more consistent approach to assessing and prioritising these matters and took steps to embed them within our 
broader risk management, governance and reporting processes.

We provide more detail on our materiality process and its outcomes on page 54.

## Assurance

We are committed to ensuring the accuracy and integrity of the information presented in this report.  
 
Our Central Sustainability Office prepares this report, supported by a rigorous internal review process, comprehensive 
management monitoring and internal controls. In line with its Board-approved mandate, the Corporate Sustainability 
Committee further reviews the report. These processes provide reasonable assurance that the information disclosed fairly 
reflects our performance and progress. We review our assurance approach annually to ensure it remains relevant and 
effective, and we are assessing the role of external assurance to enhance the credibility of our disclosures and complement our 
internal control mechanisms.

## Feedback

We encourage you to read this report and provide feedback, which will help us continuously improve our reporting approach 
and enhance our performance and governance practices. For more information about this report or to provide feedback, 
please email cso@mcb.mu

We participate in national and global industry associations and voluntary initiatives to 
advance environmental, social and governance best practices in our business activities.

## Introducing our 2026 Sustainability Report

A Message from the  
Chair of the Corporate  
Sustainability Committee

I am pleased to present MCB Group 
Limited’s 2026 Sustainability Report. 
From the perspective of the Corporate 
Sustainability Committee, the year 
reflected the Group’s continued progress 
in embedding sustainability more deeply 
across its strategy, governance and day-
to-day decision-making processes, while 
strengthening the foundations for clearer 
accountability and long-term impact.

We discussed the progress of the Group’s 
sustainable finance offering, which reflects the 
use of finance to support more inclusive, resilient 
and sustainable growth. We also discussed the 
Group’s diversity, equity and inclusion agenda, 
together with reviewing its community activities and 
funding priorities.

We approved further investment in the Livelihoods 
Carbon Funds, in particular LCF4, reinforcing our 
commitment to nature-based carbon solutions. 
We also welcomed a new partnership with the 
Odysseo Marine Foundation to protect and restore 
Mauritius’ marine ecosystems. Through our long-
standing partnerships and the work of the MCB 
Forward Foundation, more than Rs 130 million was 
invested in community support, reaching over 60 
organisations and more than 90,000 beneficiaries.

Over the past year, the operating environment 
remained dynamic, shaped by geopolitical and 
economic uncertainty, accelerating technological 
change and mounting social and environmental 
pressures. These evolving and increasingly 
interconnected forces continued to test 
organisational resilience and competitiveness, 
while raising expectations around how businesses 
respond to the needs of their employees, clients, 
communities and the wider economy in the markets 
in which we operate.

Another key focus area was the Group’s IFRS 
S1 and S2 implementation roadmap. Beyond 
responding to evolving disclosure requirements, 
this work is supporting a more systematic 
approach to identifying, assessing and prioritising 
sustainability-related risks and opportunities. It is 
also helping to build a stronger understanding of 
the interconnections between financed emissions, 
portfolio exposure and risk management.

Against this backdrop, the Group’s purpose, 
Success Beyond Numbers, remains our guiding 
compass for decision-making, helping us respond 
to these uncertainties with discipline, resilience and 
confidence. 
 
 
Progress and areas of focus 
 
As Chair of the Corporate Sustainability Committee, 
I worked alongside my fellow Committee members 
to support the Board’s Corporate Governance and 
Sustainability Committee in overseeing the Group’s 
sustainability agenda.

While this work remains relatively nascent, we 
continue to strengthen the capabilities, processes 
and governance arrangements required to 
support long-term progress. As is the case for 
many organisations globally, access to consistent, 
reliable and decision-useful data remains a 
significant challenge. Embedding sustainability 
across governance, strategy and risk management 
further requires sustained commitment to and 
investment in organisational change and capability 
development. As these capabilities mature, they 
should strengthen the link between sustainability-
related and financial information over time, 
supporting more resilient and forward-looking 
decision-making. The Committee will continue 
to oversee the IFRS S1 and S2 implementation 
roadmap as a priority in the years ahead.

Throughout the year, we monitored progress 
against the corporate sustainability programme and 
provided guidance on the implementation of key 
initiatives. We also continued to promote the inte-
gration of environmental and social considerations 
into decision-making across the Group. While the 
Group Chief Executive provides a broader account of 
the Group’s strategic priorities and business perfor-
mance later in this report, I would like to share what 
stood out for the Committee this year.

15
MCB Group Limited | Sustainability Report 2026


---
*Page 17*


The focus on strengthening governance and 
accountability extended across the Group’s broader 
sustainability agenda. During the year, the Board 
approved the Group’s Sustainability Policy, available 
on the Group’s website, formalising our commitment 
to integrate sustainability principles across our 
strategy, operations and value chain. In addition, we 
continued to monitor the frameworks, policies and 
controls supporting responsible conduct, including 
environmental, social and climate-related risk 
management, enhanced due diligence and sector-
specific risk policies.

Alongside our materiality assessment and 
stakeholder engagement processes, we continued 
to use external ESG ratings as an additional source 
of insight to identify improvement opportunities 
and benchmark our sustainability performance 
against industry peers across our local, regional and 
international operations. 
 
 
Looking ahead 
 
As we look to the coming year, the Committee will 
continue to oversee the delivery of the Group’s 
sustainability ambitions, ensuring they remain 
aligned with its commitment to use finance as a 
force for good in creating a sustainable and inclusive 
future for the customers and communities it serves. 
We also remain committed to strengthening our 
governance model to ensure it remains adaptable 
to the evolving sustainability landscape, including 
retaining the flexibility to adjust our focus as new 
risks, opportunities and stakeholder expectations 
emerge.

“ We discussed 
the progress 
of the Group’s 
sustainable 
finance offering, 
which reflects the 
use of finance 
to support more 
inclusive, resilient 
and sustainable 
growth. “

In closing, I would like to thank my fellow Committee 
members and the Board for their guidance, 
engagement and contribution throughout the 
year. I also extend my appreciation to the Group’s 
Sustainability team and to all employees involved 
in driving and implementing the various initiatives 
across the Group, as well as to our clients, partners 
and community organisations for their continued 
support and collaboration.

It is this shared vision, leadership and commitment 
that gives meaning to Success Beyond Numbers and 
guides the Group’s contribution to shared prosperity 
across the markets it serves.

## Maya Makanjee

Chair, Corporate Sustainability Committee 
MCB Group Limited

## Our Clusters and Entities

## Banking

MCB Ltd is the main subsidiary of the Group and is headquartered in Mauritius. The Bank has maintained its position as the 
market leader in the banking sector, while establishing itself as a recognised player in selected segments in Africa. It delivers 
a palette of financial products and services across its client segments as follows:

as institutional 
clients through 
tailored financing, 
transactional and 
investment solutions 
as well as syndication 
offerings.

Our network of regional hubs in Johannesburg, Nairobi, Paris, Lagos and Dubai strengthens the Group’s proximity to clients 
and business partners, supporting cross-border business development and access to opportunities across Africa and 
international markets.

The Group's banking subsidiaries in Madagascar, Maldives and Seychelles, together with its associate, Banque Française 
Commerciale Océan Indien, operating in Réunion, Mayotte and Paris, deliver a broad range of banking solutions tailored to 
the needs of their respective markets.

## Non-banking financial

The Group has entrenched its participation in non-banking financial services, with increasing prominence as a major player in 
advisory and investment solutions, leasing, factoring and microfinance.

## Other investments

The Group is involved in other business areas and ancillary investments, with dedicated structures to promote philanthropy 
and community support.

## Our Financial and Wealth Solutions

Through its banking and non-banking entities, the Group provides its clients with customised and innovative financial 
solutions as well as dedicated advice to meet their ambitions. We work closely with customers to understand their 
imperatives, challenges and priorities and deliver solutions tailored to their specific needs.

Read more about the Group’s clusters, entities and financial and wealth solutions in our 2026 MCB Group Annual Report.

## Leadership insights on sustainability

A word from the Group Head of  
Sustainability, Reputation and  
Engagement

For generations, MCB Group 
has played a meaningful part in 
supporting the development of 
Mauritius and the communities 
we serve. Today, as economic, 
environmental and social 
challenges become increasingly 
interconnected, we believe 
our role is to contribute to the 
long-term resilience of our 
home markets and support 
responsible growth and 
transition across Africa. This 
is at the heart of what Success 
Beyond Numbers means to us.

We are also looking at how 
our capital can support 
environmental and social 
outcomes beyond our lending 
activities. Building on our 
participation in Livelihoods 
Carbon Fund 3, we committed 
€3 million to Livelihoods Carbon 
Fund 4 this year. The fund aims 
to restore forests, mangroves 
and agricultural soils, improve 
the lives of more than 500,000 
people and avoid 7 to 10 million 
tonnes of CO2 e over the next  
25 years.

Our preparations for IFRS S1 
and S2 form part of this journey, 
strengthening the foundations of 
our sustainability disclosures.

Progress will not always be 
linear, and there is more to learn. 
As sustainability evolves, our 
approach must evolve with it. 
This means remaining pragmatic 
and transparent about what we 
achieve and where more needs to 
be done, while staying connected 
to realities on the ground and to 
the opportunities the transition 
creates. It also means working 
alongside our clients, colleagues, 
partners and communities to 
turn our commitments into 
measurable progress.

As a financial institution, one of 
our greatest levers is the way 
we mobilise and direct capital. 
Making a difference means 
staying close to our clients, 
understanding their realities and 
identifying where financing can 
help them transition, invest and 
grow responsibly. Having fully 
deployed our initial Rs 10 billion 
sustainable finance line across 
more than 200 projects, we 
increased it to Rs 25 billion this 
year to support more Mauritian 
businesses. Beyond Mauritius, 
we are also strengthening the 
Group’s role as a financing 
partner across Africa, supporting 
clients and projects contributing 
to the continent’s transition and 
sustainable growth.

Building resilience is also about 
people, communities and the 
heritage that connects us. 
Through the MCB Forward 
Foundation and our impact 
partnerships, we continued 
to support environmental 
protection, social development 
and community empowerment 
in Mauritius and Rodrigues. Our 
social leave programme enabled 
454 employees to contribute 
their time and skills across 22 
activities. Through the Blue 
Penny Museum, which welcomed 
28,600 visitors this year, we 
continued to preserve and share 
Mauritius’ cultural heritage while 
broadening access to it.

Our blue economy study, 
commissioned this year, is a 
good example. Bringing together 
businesses, experts and other 
stakeholders, we are exploring 
how Mauritius can unlock the 
ocean’s economic potential while 
protecting the natural capital on 
which it depends, and how finance 
can help enable this ambition. The 
study’s findings will inform our 
engagement on these opportunities 
over the coming years.

Looking ahead, our ambition 
is clear: to make sustainability 
increasingly part of how we create 
long-term value. Guided by our 
purpose and Vision 2030, we will 
continue to play our part, directing 
capital and collaborating with our 
long-standing partners to help 
build stronger economies and 
communities that can grow, adapt 
and thrive for generations to come.

These initiatives may differ in 
nature, but they reflect the same 
conviction: a stable economy, a 
healthy environment, inclusive 
communities and strong 
institutions all contribute to our 
country’s long-term resilience – 
and ultimately to MCB’s.

Understanding the impact 
associated with our financing 
is equally important. This year, 
we completed our first pilot of 
financed emissions accounting, 
bringing together colleagues 
across different lines of business 
to better understand the 
footprint of our financing. We 
also published our Sustainability 
Policy, approved by the Board 
in February 2026, providing 
a common framework for 
integrating sustainability into 
the way we finance, operate and 
engage with stakeholders.

As our actions grow, so must 
our ability to understand and 
measure their impact. This 
remains an evolving area for the 
Group, and we are strengthening 
the data, capabilities and 
processes needed to measure 
outcomes more effectively and 
direct our efforts where they can 
make the greatest difference.

Group Head of Sustainability,

Our 
Sustainability 
Approach

Our sustainability approach combines the 
systems, structures and processes that help 
embed sustainability into how we make 
decisions, set priorities and manage the 
business over time.

Our approach spans the governance structures that provide oversight 
and accountability, the strategic considerations that guide our 
sustainability priorities and the processes used to identify, assess and 
manage sustainability-related impacts, risks and opportunities that may 
affect our business and stakeholders.

Governance of Sustainability  
 
We believe that good governance is fundamental to fostering  
long-term business sustainability, creating value for our 
stakeholders and acting for the good of society.

Our Board sets the tone from the top, embedding purpose-driven 
leadership that promotes accountability, transparency, effective risk 
and performance management, robust internal control, responsible 
stakeholder engagement and ethical behaviour by all employees. Our 
Board is supported by a well-defined governance framework, which 
enables the delegation of authority and clear lines of responsibility 
while allowing the Board to retain effective control.

We provide a high-level overview of our sustainability governance 
structure on the following page, highlighting the Board and 
management committees responsible for overseeing our corporate 
sustainability programme and guiding decision-making related to 
environmental, social and governance risks.

We provide the details of these committees on the following 
pages. Further detail on our approach to environmental and social 
risk management (ESRM) and climate risk is detailed in the risk 
management section.

Introducing our Sustainability Policy 
 
MCB Group formalised its Group-wide Sustainability Policy in February 2026. This policy establishes an 
overarching framework for integrating environmental, social and governance principles into our strategy, 
operations and across the value chain. The Policy supports alignment with our purpose, Success Beyond 
Numbers, and Vision 2030, and applies across all Group subsidiaries and geographies of operation.

The Policy is owned by the CSO. It was validated by the CGSC and the Board. It is subject to formal review every 
two years, or earlier where required by material changes in regulation, stakeholder expectations or strategic 
priorities.

Our Sustainability Policy is available here.

## Evolving our sustainability governance structure

We continuously evolve our governance approach to ensure it remains responsive to our rapidly changing market 
conditions and provides adequate oversight of sustainability-related impacts, risks and opportunities across our 
value chain.

## Preparing for the IFRS Sustainability Disclosure Standards

An important part of this evolution is supporting progressive alignment with the IFRS Sustainability Disclosure Standards 
(IFRS S1 and S2). While not yet mandatory in Mauritius, alignment will help us enhance our governance and management of 
sustainability-related matters. It will further help ensure that we provide our stakeholders with comprehensive, comparable 
and consistent sustainability-related financial information.

In FY24, we established a cross-functional IFRS Working Group to coordinate this journey and drive the 
implementation of our IFRS S1 and S2 roadmap.

During the year, we refreshed our disclosure gap analysis against IFRS S1 and S2.

We further strengthened cross-functional collaboration, focusing on advancing the integration of 
sustainability- and climate-related risks within the existing risk management framework and improving the 
connectivity of sustainability-related and financial information. In addition to supporting alignment with 
IFRS S1 and S2, this work supports the disclosure requirements set out in the Bank of Mauritius Guideline 
on Climate-related and Environmental Financial Risk Management. The strengthening of our underlying 
sustainability reporting systems and processes also supports broader alignment with the GRI Standards.

We continued to develop our financed emissions calculation and reporting capabilities.

Recognising that credible, decision-useful disclosure relies on robust data, controls and skills, we invested 
in building internal capability through targeted training and technical workshops covering areas such 
as IFRS S1 and S2 and GRI reporting, climate reporting, sustainability-related risk assessment and assurance 
readiness. In parallel, we began exploring the use of a dedicated ESG data and reporting solution to 
support more consistent, controlled and audit-ready sustainability data management across the Group.

Read more about sustainability-related risk on page 53, climate risk on page 64 and financed emissions on page 116.

## Looking ahead

Our FY27 priorities are to continue closing the gaps identified through our analysis, strengthen 
governance-related disclosures, further define how sustainability-related matters are integrated into 
decision-making and progress on the integration of sustainability- and climate-related risks within 
the risk management framework. We also plan to strengthen our assurance readiness. As our data, 
methodologies and internal capabilities mature, we will continue to deepen and broaden our IFRS 
S1 and S2 disclosures on an iterative basis.

## The role of the Board

The oversight of sustainability-related matters is part of the Board’s responsibilities and members collectively work in setting 
policies and principles that govern the Group’s activities, covering areas such as:

•	
Establishing the Group’s governance-related policies and procedures

•	
Reviewing risk management, internal control and reporting systems

•	
Promoting ethical and responsible decision-making throughout the Group

•	
Embedding sound sustainability practices across the Group’s business and operations

The Board is supported by six committees with the CGSC and RMC playing the primary role in supporting the Board’s 
oversight of sustainability-related matters.

Both committees report their findings and recommendations to the Board after every scheduled meeting or at ad-hoc 
committee meetings as needed.

The CGSC assists the Board on remuneration, corporate governance, director and senior 
executive nominations and succession planning for Group senior executives and key 
management positions. Through its sub-committee, the Corporate Sustainability Committee, 
it also monitors the implementation of the Group’s corporate sustainability initiatives.

Read more about the Corporate Sustainability Committee on page 47.

The RMC assists the Board in overseeing risk mitigation strategies and assesses the risk 
management processes for the Group and its subsidiaries. Its sustainability-related oversight 
focuses on climate-related financial risks and environmental and social risks, as further 
described in the Committee’s responsibilities.

Read more about the RMC on page 48.

The remaining Board committees also contribute to effective oversight across their respective areas of 
responsibility:

The Audit Committee assists the Board in overseeing compliance with applicable laws, 
regulations, policies and procedures.

The Cyber and Technology Risk Committee assists the Board in overseeing technology, 
cybersecurity and data privacy risks across the Group.

The Corporate Strategy Committee assists the Board in overseeing the strategic direction of 
the Group and its subsidiaries and assesses performance against set objectives.

The Supervisory and Monitoring Committee assists the Board in overseeing the overall 
management of the Group and its subsidiaries, including the deployment of sustainability 
initiatives.

More information on the role of the Board committees is available in our 2026 MCB Group Annual Report.

## Our Board in FY26

The Board comprises a diverse mix of the skills, knowledge and experience needed to encourage debate and support good 
decision-making and strategy execution. To encourage a broader range of perspectives during deliberations, the Board 
diversity profile further includes different genders, cultures and ages.

As at 30 June 2026, the Board consisted of 10 members.

## Focus on sustainability skills and training

We are committed to equipping our Board members with the necessary knowledge, skills and expertise to 
effectively oversee and respond to sustainability-related impacts, risks and opportunities.

Director development is supported through a comprehensive induction programme for newly appointed directors and an 
ongoing training programme to enhance the Board’s understanding of the evolving business landscape. Directors may also 
request specific training to support the effective discharge of their responsibilities.

During FY26, Board members continued to deepen their understanding of sustainability-related 
matters through targeted training.  
 
12 Directors attended a seminar on Just Transition, facilitated by the United Nations Environment 
Programme Finance Initiative (UNEP FI). Directors also completed in-house e-learning on key aspects of 
responsible behaviour, including anti-money laundering, counter terrorism and proliferation financing, 
as well as data privacy and cybersecurity. Training was supported by an online assessment to measure 
Director understanding.

Read more about the Just Transition training and other initiatives to promote sustainability awareness and understanding 
among our employees on page 126.

## Our Sustainability Approach

## The role of the nominated Board committees

## Corporate Governance and Sustainability Committee (CGSC)

The CGSC oversees the Group’s corporate sustainability programme, supported by the Corporate Sustainability 
Committee, a sub-committee that assists it in fulfilling this mandate.

## Corporate Sustainability Committee (CSC)

The CSC supports the CGSC by monitoring progress on the Group’s corporate sustainability programme and overseeing 
the implementation of relevant sustainability initiatives. Its responsibilities also include supporting the integration of 
environmental and social considerations into decision-making. The CSC reports its findings and recommendations to the 
CGSC after every scheduled meeting or at ad hoc committee meetings as needed.

## Membership and relevant skills and competencies

•	
Maya Makanjee (Chairperson): Independent Non-Executive Director of MCB Group

•	
Jayananda Nirsimloo: Chairman of MCB Group

•	
Simon Walker: Chairman of MCB Ltd

•	
Johanne Hague: Independent Non-Executive Director of MCB Ltd

•	
Yvan Legris: Non-Executive Director of MCB Group

•	
Jean Michel Ng Tseung: Executive Director of MCB Group

•	
Thierry Hebraud: Executive Director of MCB Ltd

•	
Dipak Chummun: Executive Director of MCB Group

The committee’s diverse composition reflects the breadth of expertise needed to effectively oversee the execution 
of the Group’s corporate sustainability programme. Its members bring extensive experience in banking and 
financial services, with a deep understanding of African and regional social and environmental development 
issues. Members also have specific skills related to governance, corporate affairs and stakeholder engagement, 
ensuring a holistic approach to sustainability.

## Risk Monitoring Committee (RMC)

The RMC oversees sustainability-related risks within the Group’s broader risk management framework, with a particular 
focus on environmental and social risks and climate-related financial risks.

In relation to ESRM, the RMC receives quarterly reporting on ESRM-eligible exposures across business segments and sectors, 
including updates on high E&S-risk exposures and sensitive transactions. In addition, material climate-related risk issues 
must be escalated to the RMC.

In support of its oversight responsibilities, the RMC reviews the Group’s E&S Risk Policy and the Bank’s Climate-related 
Financial Risk Policy on an annual basis and recommends their approval to the Board.

More information on the RMC’s composition and activities during the year is available in our 2026 MCB Group Annual Report.  
Read more about our approach to risk management from page 59.

## The role of the management committees

## Sustainability Strategic Council (SSC)

Our SSC is the highest management-level committee overseeing our Group’s corporate sustainability programme. 
 
Membership, role and responsibilities

The SSC comprises our Chief Executives (Bank and Group), Group Chief Finance Officer, cross-functional Heads of SBUs 
(including Human Resources, Risk, Global Structured Finance and SRD) and our CSO.

The SSC is responsible for implementing our Board-approved corporate sustainability programme. This includes assessing 
proposed initiatives to ensure they align with the programme and address our key economic and sustainability-related 
matters. The SSC also oversees action plans and approves and monitors the project budgets.

## Key matters covered in FY26

The SSC met four times and covered the following topics related to our corporate sustainability programme:

## Executive Credit Committee (ECC)

The ECC sanctions credit requests and oversees the management of credit risk portfolios within MCB Ltd, including  
oversight on ESRM recommendations and breaches. The ECC ensures that environmental and social risks as well as 
 potential reputational risks are adequately considered, alongside other credit risks, for decision-making on deals/
transactions. The ECC comprises the Bank Chief Executive, Chief Risk Officer, Head of Credit Management and the  
Heads of SBUs, including Global Coverage and Global Structured Finance.

Read more about our ESRM escalation process on page 61. More information on the ECC is available in our 2026 MCB Group Annual Report.

## Our Sustainability Approach

## The role of dedicated teams

Dedicated teams provide additional support and enable the day-to-day delivery of our corporate sustainability programme 
by driving approved initiatives and embedding sustainability-related activities, including environmental, social and climate 
risk management, into business operations. These functions report to the relevant management and/or Board committee for 
oversight and strategic alignment.

## Central Sustainability Office (CSO)

Our CSO is responsible for developing and driving the implementation of the Group’s corporate sustainability programme 
across three key workstreams. The CSO reports to the Group Head of Sustainability, Reputation and Engagement (SRE), who 
in turn reports to our Group Chief Executive.

## Environmental and Social Risk Management (ESRM) team

Our ESRM team sits in the Credit management BU within our Risk SBU. It is responsible for conducting E&S risk due diligence 
on the Bank’s ESRM eligible financing activities. The team escalates its analysis, recommendations and proposed conditions 
on related E&S matters to the Executive Credit Committee for decision-making. The ESRM team implements our E&S Risk 
Policy, including the identification and mitigation of E&S risks and monitoring of E&S-related conditions in the contractual 
agreement of transactions. The team also provides training to relevant internal stakeholders.

Additionally, the ESRM team reports quarterly to the RMC to provide visibility on ESRM eligible exposures by business 
segment and sectors, with a focus on high E&S risk exposures and sensitive files.

## Climate Risk

Climate Risk leads the integration of climate-related financial risk into the Bank’s risk management framework. The team 
develops climate risk methodologies, performs portfolio analysis and provides guidance on physical and transition risks. The 
team sits within Financial Risk and reports to the Head of Financial Risk, who reports to the Chief Risk Officer.

## Climate-related Financial Risk Policy

The Bank introduced a Climate-related Financial Risk Policy approved by the Board in May 2026, 
which sets out the governance arrangements for managing climate-related financial risks. This 
includes the roles and responsibilities of the Board, senior management and relevant internal 
functions. Roles and responsibilities for climate-related financial risks are defined in alignment 
with the Three Lines of Defence model.

Below is the Bank’s established governance framework to cater for climate-related risk management considerations.

## Sustainable Finance Taskforce (SFT)

Last year, we strengthened governance and oversight of the Group’s sustainable finance agenda. The SFT supports the 
development and execution of sustainable finance priorities across the Group, including transition planning, capacity 
building and oversight of key initiatives.

During FY26, the SFT focused on reviewing the Group’s sustainable finance performance and existing offerings, considered 
the increase in the sustainable finance line from Rs 10 billion to Rs 25 billion and explored opportunities to further 
digitalise sustainable finance data. Other focus areas included strengthening impact measurement alongside developing a 
methodology to assess clients’ ESG maturity. In parallel, the SFT reviewed progress on the implementation of the UNEP FI 
PRB, including the validation of related targets.

The sponsors of the SFT are the Head of SRE, Global Head of Structured Finance and the Group Chief Risk Officer. The SFT 
reports to the SSC. The SFT meets quarterly and comprises members of the Corporate and Institutional segment, namely 
the Sustainable Finance Desk, Global Head of Coverage, Global Head of Structured Finance, Head of Specialised Finance and 
Head of Mauritian and Regional Corporates, the CSO, as well as the Head of MCB Overseas.

to economic resilience, social inclusion and community

pursue to take a leading role in creating prosperity 
and resilience for its stakeholders. In the years since, 
sustainability considerations have increasingly shaped how 
we serve our clients, structure financing, allocate capital,

and communities.

## Our approach to sustainability materiality

As a leading bank in Mauritius and a prominent regional financial services player, MCB recognises that its long-term success 
is closely connected to the resilience and prosperity of its clients, employees, communities and the economies in which it 
operates. Understanding our sustainability impacts, dependencies, risks and opportunities helps us identify the matters that 
are most significant to our stakeholders and our business.

## Our sustainability materiality process

## Step 1: Identify

In FY24, we undertook an extensive sustainability materiality analysis to identify the impacts relevant to MCB Ltd 
across its activities and value chain. The analysis was guided by the GRI Standards. It also provided a foundation for 
MCB’s ongoing work towards alignment with the IFRS Sustainability Disclosure Standards.

## The analysis differentiated between:

•	
Direct impacts: outcomes arising principally from our own operations and activities, over which we have a 
relatively high degree of influence and control.

•	
Indirect impacts: outcomes associated with our products, services or financed activities, which we can 
influence but do not fully control.

The analysis involved inclusive and participatory engagement with more than 400 internal and external 
stakeholders, including Board members, employees, investors, retail and business clients, corporate clients, 
suppliers, authorities and regulatory bodies, business partners, correspondent banks, media representatives and 
civil-society organisations.

Engagement was conducted through facilitated discussions and an online survey and was supplemented by 
market, industry and sustainability research.

## Step 2: Assess and prioritise

As part of our FY24 sustainability materiality analysis, we assessed and prioritised our sustainability matters by 
applying the concept of ‘double materiality’.

Accordingly, we considered our most significant impacts on the environment and people (impact materiality) 
as well as the significant sustainability-related dependencies, risks, and opportunities that could reasonably be 
expected to affect MCB’s prospects in the short, medium, and long term (financial materiality).

Through this assessment, we identified MCB’s material sustainability matters, detailed on the following 
pages.

The complete methodology, stakeholder engagement outcomes, scoring approach and detailed materiality results 
are detailed in our online Materiality Analysis for MCB Ltd.

Our  Materiality Analysis can be accessed on our website.

## Step 3: Integrate

Given the relatively recent introduction of the IFRS Sustainability Disclosure Standards, much of our work in FY25 
focused on assessing the alignment and consistency of our materiality analysis with the Standards and emerging 
implementation practice, and we continue to evolve our approach as our understanding matures.

At the same time, we believe our FY24 materiality analysis provided an important starting point for understanding 
the sustainability matters most relevant to the Group and its stakeholders. In FY25, we therefore also focused on 
further unpacking our sustainability impacts to identify and assess the associated risks and opportunities. During 
FY26, we made headway developing a more consistent approach to assessing and prioritising these matters, while 
considering how they should be integrated into our broader risk management framework and related governance 
and reporting processes.

54
MCB Group Limited | Sustainability Report 2026

## Our Sustainability Approach

## Our work during the year focused on:

Strengthening cross-functional collaboration and shared understanding, including building a deeper 
understanding across the Sustainability, Reputation & Engagement (SRE) and Risk SBUs of the Group’s 
strategic risk management process, relevant sustainability reporting requirements (including GRI and 
the IFRS Sustainability Disclosure Standards) and sustainability-related dependencies, impacts, risks and 
opportunities.

## 1

Advancing the assessment and prioritisation of sustainability-related risks. We applied the Group’s 
existing risk methodology to assess and prioritise the broad set of sustainability-related risks identified in 
FY25 and refine these into a more focused and coherent set of priority risks. This work remains in progress 
and considers these risks through both financial and impact materiality lenses.

## 2

Progressing integration and governance, including ongoing work to finalise the governance framework 
for integrating sustainability-related risks into the Group’s Enterprise Risk Management framework. The 
current prioritisation work is being jointly overseen by the SRE and Risk SBUs, with engagement of the 
relevant Risk Owners forming an important next step.

## 3

Refining our approach to emerging interconnected risks. We recognise that certain matters, particularly 
climate change, biodiversity and water-related risks, may require more tailored assessment approaches due 
to their location-specific characteristics, data requirements and longer-term dynamics. These considerations 
are not yet fully reflected in the methodology applied to our broader sustainability-related risk assessment, 
and we are continuing to develop an appropriate approach to assessing and prioritising them more 
comprehensively.

## 4

Progressing the identification and assessment of sustainability-related opportunities, with work 
under way to prioritise identified opportunities and further refine the governance arrangements for their 
management and oversight.

## 5

## Next steps

In the year ahead, we will complete the prioritisation of our sustainability-related risks and opportunities. We will also 
develop a more tailored methodology for assessing interconnected climate change, biodiversity and water-related risks.

We will then focus on operationalisation and ownership to further the integration of sustainability-related risks into our 
Enterprise Risk Management framework. In parallel, we will finalise the governance approach for sustainability-related 
opportunities and continue to strengthen the capabilities required to support the management of sustainability-related 
risks and opportunities over time.

Key takeaways from our materiality analysis and how these insights impact our strategy and business activities

## The most significant environmental impacts arise through financing:

## 1

The environmental impacts associated with MCB’s own operations and are managed through our operational 
environmental programme. However, the scale and nature of MCB’s wider environmental influence arise 
principally through the activities it finances. This reinforces the importance of environmental and social risk 
management, climate-risk assessment, sector-level due diligence, client engagement and the continued 
development of sustainable finance solutions.

## Social and human rights impacts extend across the value chain:

## 2

Matters such as child labour and forced or compulsory labour arise primarily through indirect exposure to 
clients, suppliers and other business relationships rather than through MCB’s direct operations. This highlights 
the importance of appropriate screening, due diligence, contractual requirements and escalation processes.

## Economic resilience and inclusion remain central to MCB’s role:

## 3

MCB’s financial resilience and economic contribution support its ability to provide finance, facilitate 
investment, enable entrepreneurship and contribute to sustainable local and regional economic development. 
The assessment also highlights the significance of MCB’s market presence, procurement practices and broader 
indirect economic impacts.

## Trust depends on responsible conduct and customer protection:

## 4

Business ethics, fair market conduct, responsible communication, customer outcomes and data privacy are 
important foundations of stakeholder trust. These matters are increasingly relevant as financial services 
become more digital, interconnected and data dependent.

## People and organisational capabilities underpin implementation:

## 5

Employee well-being, skills development, labour relations, diversity, equity and inclusion influence MCB’s 
ability to execute its strategy and respond effectively to evolving sustainability expectations. Continued 
capacity building across relevant employee groups will be important as MCB embeds sustainability 
considerations more deeply into risk management, client engagement and decision-making.

## Our Sustainability Approach

## Risk Management

Responsible risk management supports the resilience of our business and reinforces our ability to create 
sustainable, long-term value.

We maintain a robust risk management and compliance framework, underpinned by sound governance and a strong 
risk culture across the Group. Our ambition is to ensure that we effectively manage our key risks while identifying and 
responding to business opportunities that create value for our clients, customers and communities.

A key component of this is identifying, assessing and integrating material sustainability-related risks within our broader risk 
management framework. During FY26, we made steady progress in this area, building on the outcomes of our sustainability 
materiality assessment. We will provide further insight into our progress in future reports as this work matures.

Further details on our risk management strategy and framework are available in our 2026 MCB Group Annual Report. Read more about our work to 
identify, assess and integrate sustainability-related risks and opportunities on page 54.

## E&S risks

In line with our purpose, we aim to contribute to building a sustainable and inclusive future in our home markets and across 
Africa. By properly embedding ESRM into our credit cycle and decision-making, we strive to encourage responsible business 
practices, promote positive impacts and reduce potential negative impacts on people, communities and the environment.

## Our E&S Risk Policy

Our E&S Risk Policy highlights our engagement to ensure that our exposure to E&S risks through our financing activities is 
properly identified and managed through adequate risk management framework and standards.

Primarily, the Bank has developed an Exclusion List, approved by the Board, for activities having damaging impacts on the 
environment and human rights. The policy also stipulates the type of transactions considered eligible for Environmental and 
Social Risk Assessment, which covers all sectors within our risk appetite.

The Bank is also committed to capturing emerging E&S risks such as biodiversity, climate, gender and working conditions, 
present in sectors like agriculture, oil and gas, metals and minerals, manufacturing, property development and renewable 
energy among others. Irrespective of the project costs or loan amount, projects identified to be concerned with those 
emerging risks will undergo an ESRM assessment for proper risk mitigation measures.

This year, the Bank approved Sector Risk policies for the Oil and Gas and Metals and Mining sectors, which include more 
robust and enhanced E&S risk management practices when conducting E&S due diligence for those sectors.

The scope of application of the E&S Risk Policy has been established with financing types and financial threshold to reflect 
our local and international operating context. This approach has also been adopted for financing types or products targeted 
for corporate, SME and MME clients, since the ESRM process also applies to all sustainable financing products, corporate and 
government loans as well as unfunded facilities. Since 2020, the E&S Risk Policy has been reviewed on an annual basis.

As per its Board-approved mandate, the RMC reviews our E&S Risk Policy annually, with the latest review 
conducted in May 2026. The RMC further recommends our E&S Risk Policy to the Board for approval.  
The main amendments brought to the E&S Risk Policy in FY26 are:

•	 Alignment of the ESRM practices with Vision 2030.

•	 Improved details for the scope of application in terms of sectors covered within our risk appetite and 
clarification required for working capital and/or trade finance facilities. The scope of application was enhanced 
to include financing types such as prepayment for oil and gas and metals and mining and corporate bonds.

•	 Requirements to conduct climate change risk assessments on Category A and B (high-risk) projects 
to assess the associated physical and transition risks, as well as the projects’ alignment to relevant climate 
commitments of the host country.

•	 Introduced due diligence requirements for responsible sourcing for trade finance facilities related to the 
Metals and Mining sector.

Our E&S Risk Policy, including our Exclusion List, as well as the sector risk policies are available on our website.

59
MCB Group Limited | Sustainability Report 2026

*Page 61*


## Environmental and Social Risk Management process

In line with our objectives and the growing importance of managing E&S risks to businesses, we developed our ESRM process 
to systematically identify, assess, manage and monitor the E&S risks to which we are exposed through our lending activities. 
The ESRM process and procedures have been developed to assess different E&S risk category of projects and have been 
integrated into the overall credit cycle of the Bank, from initiation to monitoring phases.

## Environmental and Social Risk Due Diligence

## Compliance with the MCB Exclusion List

The Bank has developed a guideline for the coverage team, alongside the ESRM team, to conduct a preliminary E&S 
screening to ensure compliance with the MCB Exclusion List for new clients or projects. This includes, for example, projects 
involving forced labour, destruction of critical habitats or the production of hazardous materials, among others. Once clients 
or projects are found to comply with the MCB exclusion list, we conduct our ESRM due diligence following the steps  
outlined below.

## Eligibility

We assess whether transactions are eligible for the E&S risk due diligence based on the scope of application of our E&S Risk Policy.

## Risk identification and categorisation

Risk identification is an integral part of our ESRM process which we conduct at various stages of the credit cycle. We apply the 
criteria defined by the International Finance Corporation (IFC) and the Equator Principles to categorise projects according to 
the magnitude of their potential impacts.

## These categories are described as follows:

Category A: 
Category B: 
Category C:

Projects with potential limited 
adverse E&S risks and/or impacts 
that are few in number, generally 
site-specific, largely reversible 
and readily addressed through 
mitigation measures.

Projects with potential significant 
adverse E&S risks and/or impacts 
that are diverse, irreversible or 
unprecedented.

Projects with minimal or no adverse 
E&S risks or impacts.

## E&S risk assessment

All eligible ESRM projects undergo E&S risk due diligence process to establish the compliance level of the project, existing 
assets and activities if related to the project, by reviewing the following:

•	 All applicable environmental and social laws, regulatory frameworks and necessary permits

•	 Requirements under the Equator Principles, including the United Nations Guiding Principles on Business and Human 
Rights and Climate Change Risk Assessment

## •	 Applicable IFC Performance Standards

## •	 Industry-specific human rights, environment and health and safety guidelines

The degree of E&S risk due diligence is determined by the category of the project. For Category A and high-risk Category B 
projects, the E&S risks will be assessed by a Bank-appointed independent environmental and social consultant with a defined 
scope of work covering the above requirements. Medium or low Category B and Category C projects will be assessed against 
the same criteria by the internal ESRM team. Additionally, all ESRM eligible projects will also be assessed on their sector-
specific E&S risks using available E&S risk identification tools. With the support of Proparco Technical Assistance, the Bank 
has recently developed Climate, Biodiversity and Gender Risk Assessment tools which are used during the due diligence 
process.

The Bank can also apply environmental and social risk due diligence on projects with specific E&S risk concerns, irrespective 
of whether the project falls within the scope of application. In some cases, a simplified E&S review may be applied.

The due diligence process may necessitate specific preventative or mitigation measures to manage or reduce potential E&S 
impacts. The Bank will develop the adequate environmental and social action plan, specifying key deliverables and timelines, 
to bring the project into compliance with the applicable standards and the Bank’s requirements.

60
MCB Group Limited | Sustainability Report 2026

## Our Sustainability Approach

## Escalation process

All credit requests eligible for an ESRM process include the findings of the E&S risk due diligence, the adequate E&S 
recommendations and conditions, and are escalated for decision-making to the Executive Credit Committee (ECC). The ECC 
will review and sanction the requests based on credit risk alongside reputational and E&S risks, including climate, biodiversity 
and gender risks, as follows:

## •	 Approve the transaction

## •	 Reject the transaction

## •	 Approve the transaction subject to amendments to be brought to the request

Upon approval of the request, the relevant environmental and social conditions and covenants are included in the 
contractual agreements with the clients.

Read more about the Bank’s ESRM governance, including the role and composition of the ECC, on page 48.

## Environmental and social covenant monitoring

The E&S covenants and the E&S performance (Environmental and Social Action Plan and independent E&S monitoring report) 
of the project are monitored throughout the loan tenor, as per the contractual agreement. These are often supported by 
ongoing client engagement and site visits. Any covenant breach is escalated to the ECC.

For higher E&S risk exposures, the ESRM team monitors the ongoing E&S risks and provides their input to the ECC.  
Our higher E&S risk sectors comprise:

•	
Oil and Gas
•	
Metals and Minerals
•	
Real-estate projects located in environmentally sensitive areas
•	
Agriculture
•	
Construction

As at end of FY26, 312 ESRM eligible disbursed financing were being monitored for their E&S performance.

## Our E&S risk incidents

## Ongoing monitoring of FY25 incidents

Following E&S risk incidents encountered on one cross-border client last year, in which MCB is a participant in a syndicated 
facility, the Bank continued its E&S monitoring during FY26. While there has been some improvement on the overall 
Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) and Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP) issued for the 
project, the Bank, together with the group of lenders, continued to monitor remedial actions as well as the project’s overall 
Environmental and Social performance through the appointed independent E&S consultant. Quarterly reports are also 
provided to RMC.

## FY26 E&S risk incidents

During FY26, the Bank encountered three minor and two major environmental and social risk incidents on one local and four 
cross-border clients. All incidents, mainly related to health and safety on a cross-border gas plant, road accidents involving 
contracted logistics services of a cross-border client and accidental chemical spillage in a local oil storage facility, were 
reported to the Bank, with ongoing follow-ups conducted on the investigations and progress against the identified remedial 
actions.

## ESRM training

A key objective of the annual review of the E&S Risk Policy is to raise awareness on the scope and application of our policy. 
It is usually followed by compulsory training rolled out to relevant stakeholders, including client-facing, legal, climate risk, 
documentation, compliance and credit risk teams among others, followed by an assessment.

## 91% completion rate among employees assigned E&S Risk Policy training

## 2,790 employees completed the training in FY26 (FY25: 1,448)

As part of the Proparco Technical Assistance programme, 75 employees involved in the credit value chain and the leadership 
team received training from external E&S consultants, covering:

•	
The importance of E&S risks for financial institutions, with case studies on the Cocoa, Renewable Energy and Oil and  
Gas sectors.

•	
Frameworks for ESRM, with a focus on the IFC Performance Standards.

•	
Enhancement of MCB’s ESRM process via the use of the Climate, Biodiversity and Gender Risk assessment tools.

Through the same partnership, the ESRM team benefitted from KfW DEG training on labour, environmental, health and 
safety and supply chain issues. The team continued their learning journey on E&S risks in the Metals and Minerals sector.

We continued to ensure that any new employees joining our ESRM team received training on the technical aspects of 
applying our ESRM process and integrating it into the credit cycle.

## Our Sustainability Approach

## Our ESRM process in action

## Case study 1: Promoting biodiversity conservation

Projects with specific biodiversity concerns undergo additional due diligence, focusing on the country-specific 
regulations related to preservation of biodiversity/sensitive areas as well as the relevant IFC Performance 
Standards, namely PS1 (Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts), PS3 
(Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention) and PS6 (Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management 
of Living Natural Resources).

Any pertinent risk identified will follow the ESRM process, including the following:

1.	
Categorisation of the project as high E&S risk (Category A).

2.	
Appointment of external E&S consultants with a defined scope of work around IFC Performance Standards 
and a strong focus on biodiversity assessment.

3.	
Adequate escalation of ESRM recommendations and conditions to the ECC.

4.	
More stringent post-disbursement monitoring by the appointed independent E&S consultants and the 
internal ESRM team.

5.	
Ongoing engagement with the borrower to ensure progress on proposed mitigants.

## Case study 2: MCB acts as the lead E&S bank on a cross-border transaction

MCB participated in a syndicated facility to finance an oil and gas production asset in a non-OECD African 
country. The project was categorised as high E&S risk (Category A) given its sector and activity.

Being the only institutional signatory to the Equator Principles among the group of lenders, MCB acted as the 
leading bank for the application of the requirements of the Equator Principles and MCB’s E&S Risk Policy and 
standards. The Bank engaged with E&S consultants from its approved list, and the final selection of the E&S 
consultant was made in consultation with MCB, the lenders and the borrower.

Once the consultant was appointed, the Bank conducted several workshops with the consultant and client to 
ensure that the Environmental and Social Due Diligence, including the site visit, ran smoothly and in accordance 
with MCB’s E&S standards. The final report was successfully issued with a clear improvement plan in specific 
areas.

## Looking ahead

Over the past two years, under the Group Risk Management mandate, risk management practices 
have been standardised across MCB Group and its banking subsidiaries. Building on this progress, 
we plan to integrate the ESRM function into the operations of our overseas banking subsidiaries, 
starting with MCB Madagascar.

As part of Proparco’s Technical Assistance programme, we will develop a plan to conduct an external 
assessment of the ESRM process.

## Climate risk

The Bank continued to advance the integration of climate-related 
financial risks into its risk management framework, with a focus 
on strengthening governance, methodologies and analytical 
capabilities. 
 
Key developments include:

•	
Strengthening of governance framework, including approval of a 
dedicated Climate-related Financial Risk Policy.

•	
Development of risk management methodologies, including 
implementation of physical risk assessment capabilities using a 
platform provided by an internationally recognised service provider, 
and enhancement of the transition risk assessment methodology.

•	
Progressive integration of climate risk considerations into existing 
risk management processes, such as the Internal Capital Adequacy 
Assessment Process (ICAAP).

## General approach and objectives

Climate risk may directly or indirectly affect banks through exposures to 
customers, resulting in financial and non-financial impacts. At MCB, we 
assess the impact of climate change on financial risks through physical 
and transition risk factors.

## Physical risk:

## Transition risk:

This refers to potential 
losses and economic 
costs which may arise due 
to significant and rapid 
policy changes, disruptive 
technology development 
or market sentiment shifts 
as part of the transition 
towards a lower carbon 
economy.

This refers to potential 
losses and economic 
costs which may arise due 
to increased severity of 
extreme weather events 
such as cyclones and floods 
(acute risks) and longer term 
shifts in climate patterns 
such as sustained higher 
temperatures and rising sea 
levels (chronic risks).

64
MCB Group Limited | Sustainability Report 2026


---
*Page 66*


## Our Sustainability Approach

## Measuring, monitoring and management

The Bank has established methodologies to identify, assess and monitor both climate-related physical and transition risks 
across its portfolio. Physical risk assessments are performed using a platform provided by an internationally recognised 
service provider that evaluates current natural hazard exposures and projected climate-related risks under selected IPCC 
scenarios across short-, medium- and long-term time horizons.

To assess transition risk, the Bank has developed an internal methodology that evaluates the potential impact of climate 
transition pathways on its wholesale portfolio. The approach incorporates selected NGFS5 scenarios and considers the 
potential impact of carbon pricing across different time horizons. Alongside exploring the integration of the financed 
emissions calculation being undertaken under the PCAF framework, the transition risk methodology is being further 
enhanced to capture additional transmission channels.

Read more about our application of the PCAF methodology on page 116.

The Bank runs climate-related physical and transition risk assessments on its in-scope portfolio, covering a range of 
sectors and geographies. These assessments provide insights into potential risk concentrations and support the ongoing 
enhancement of climate risk management capabilities.

The Bank continues to strengthen its climate risk data, methodologies and analytical capabilities to improve the accuracy 
and coverage of risk assessments. Climate-related financial risks are being progressively embedded into existing risk 
management processes, including through the incorporation of a climate-related scenario in the ICAAP stress testing exercise 
to assess potential impacts on the Bank’s risk profile and capital adequacy over the planning horizon. This work supports the 
Bank’s broader IFRS S1 and S2 implementation roadmap, particularly the progressive strengthening of climate-related risk 
management, data and disclosure capabilities.

Read more about our IFRS S1 and S2 implementation roadmap on page 43.

The Bank has also included climate risk considerations into its sector-specific policies for Oil and Gas and Metals and 
Minerals and is implementing a climate risk screening questionnaire as part of its ESRM process.

Read more about our ESRM process on page 60.

## Limitations

Climate risk measurement methodologies continue to evolve across the financial industry and are subject to inherent 
uncertainty, including variations in modelling approaches, assumptions and data inputs. The Bank’s assessments are 
currently constrained by limitations in the availability, completeness and quality of certain climate-related data. In particular, 
physical risk assessments are dependent on geospatial information relating to clients’ operations and assets, while transition 
risk assessments rely primarily on estimated emissions data given client-specific greenhouse gas information is not readily 
available.

Accordingly, current assessments are intended to provide directional insights into potential climate-related risk exposures 
and areas of vulnerability rather than precise measurements of financial impact. The Bank continues to enhance its 
data, methodologies and analytical capabilities and expects its climate-related disclosures and assessments to become 
increasingly robust as these capabilities mature.

## Looking ahead

Recognising that climate risk measurement remains an evolving field, with methodologies and assumptions continuing to 
develop across the industry, the Bank is strengthening its data, governance and analytical capabilities to support the future 
implementation of robust and meaningful climate-related metrics and targets. These indicators will be used to monitor 
climate-related risk exposures and inform risk management and strategic decision-making. As set out by the Bank’s Climate-
related Financial Risk Policy, climate risk indicators will be monitored regularly and reported to the RMC on a half-yearly 
basis. The RMC further oversees the progressive development and integration of climate-related financial considerations into 
the Bank’s broader risk management framework.

5The Network for Greening the Financial System is a global group of over 150 central banks and financial supervisors. Launched in December 2017, 
it helps the financial sector manage climate and nature risks.

## Sustainable Finance

## Our sustainable finance framework

Our sustainable finance framework provides a consistent basis for identifying and classifying eligible green and social 
financing activities. Developed with leading  banks, it reflects recognised international principles and market practice and 
supports the continued development of our sustainable finance offering.

## Our sustainable finance framework at a glance:

Our green 
financing criteria:

Our social  
financing criteria:

•	
Renewable energy 
•	
Energy efficiency 
•	
Green buildings 
•	
Clean transportation 
•	
Sustainable water and wastewater management 
•	
Pollution prevention and control  
(including circular economy) 
•	
Climate change adaptation and resilience

•	
Employment generation and programmes 
•	
Access to essential services 
•	
Affordable housing and basic infrastructure 
•	
Food security and sustainable food systems 
•	
Preservation of cultural heritage

We mapped the SDGs to our eligible green and social financing criteria. The financing 
objectives of our sustainable finance framework aim to provide a meaningful contribution to 
these global goals.

Read more about deals that demonstrate this contribution on page 74.

We developed the framework in line with relevant industry standards, market principles and international best 
practice:

•	
International Capital Market Association (ICMA) Green Bond Principles 2021 (with June 2022 Appendix I)
•	
ICMA Social Bond Principles 2023
•	
ICMA Sustainability Bond Guidelines 2021
•	
Loan Market Association (LMA) 2023 Green Loan Principles
•	
LMA Social Loan Principles 2023

An independent third-party provided a Second Party Opinion on our sustainable finance framework, confirming alignment 
with international best practices and principles, including the ICMA’s Green, Social and Sustainability Bond principles.

We established a robust and collaborative internal process for project evaluation and selection:

•	
The Coverage team conducts initial screening against the Exclusion List and Customer Acceptance Policy.
•	
The CSO validates whether a proposed transaction meets the framework’s eligibility criteria.
•	
The ESRM team assesses compliance with the E&S Risk Policy.

Broader oversight of our sustainable finance agenda is provided through the Sustainable Finance Taskforce (SFT). 
The SFT supports the development and execution of sustainable finance priorities across the Group, including 
capacity building and oversight of key initiatives.

Read more about the ESRM team on page 49 and the SFT’s role, composition and FY26 activities on page 50.
Our sustainable finance framework is available online.

During the year, ongoing collaboration between the CSO and Coverage team supported knowledge sharing and 
the identification of potential sustainable finance opportunities within client segments. Broader sustainability 
training further helped strengthen sustainable finance knowledge, including a workshop on Just Transition.

Read more about how we are building sustainability awareness among employees on page 125.

## Our sustainable finance offerings

Through our sustainable financing activities, we seek to enable responsible, inclusive growth and strengthen economic 
and environmental resilience. In Mauritius, our growing sustainable finance offer is helping to channel financing towards 
priorities identified in the third Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 3.0), which sets out the country’s plans to 
contribute to global climate goals. Our financing has supported renewable energy, clean transportation, water and 
wastewater infrastructure, climate resilient agriculture, more resource efficient buildings and waste management.

## Our sustainable finance offerings for MCB Group comprise of:

01. Sustainable loan 
Available to both large companies and MSMEs, supporting local entrepreneurship 
and business investment in solutions addressing Mauritius’ environmental and 
social challenges.

02. Sustainable trade finance 
Comprises trade import and supply chain finance. Trade import consists of 
financing for eligible imported equipment and goods. Our approach aligns with 
international practice, including the International  Chamber of Commerce guidelines 
on sustainable trade. Supply chain finance extends sustainable financing across 
clients’ supply chains, supporting eligible businesses whose activities contribute to 
environmental stewardship and socio-economic development.

## MCB Ltd’s sustainable finance line

In April 2023, the Bank announced a dedicated Rs 10 billion sustainable finance line to support the growth of scalable 
financial solutions and help address environmental and social challenges in Mauritius. The line was intended to be fully 
deployed by 30 June 2027 but was fully utilised in FY26. Our sustainable finance line comprises sustainable loan, sustainable 
trade finance and MCB Ltd’ subscriptions to labelled instruments, supporting a range of green and social financing activities 
in Mauritius.

This larger facility will give us more capacity to continue supporting businesses of 
different sizes in accessing financing for eligible green and social activities. As we 
scale our offering, we will continue to engage with clients to identify and develop 
sustainable financing opportunities that respond to their needs and support their 
transition journeys.

announced an increase in the facility to Rs 25 billion in July 2026.

## Sustainable finance case studies

The following case studies illustrate how financing provided through our sustainable offerings is supporting positive 
environmental and social outcomes across Mauritius.

## Contributing to local food resilience

## Alignment with relevant SDGs:

## Capella

## Target 2.4

## Target 8.2

Mauritius relies on imported mushrooms to meet domestic demand, creating an opportunity to improve local 
food security and reduce dependence on imported produce.

MCB Ltd provided a sustainable loan to support Capella’s Mushroom Project, aimed at establishing the country’s 
first commercial-scale production of fresh white button and other mushroom varieties. The financing supported 
the development of controlled-environment farming infrastructure and technology required to produce local high-
quality mushrooms consistently throughout the year. The project is expected to reduce reliance on imports, create 
skilled employment opportunities and contribute to establishing local food value chains.

## Greener logistics infrastructure

## Alignment with relevant SDGs:

## Logiparks Ltd

Target 11.6
Target 9.4

## Target 13.2

As demand grows for modern logistics and warehousing infrastructure, there is an increasing need for new 
industrial developments to be more resource efficient.

MCB Ltd provided Logiparks Riche Terre Ltd with a sustainable loan to finance the development of three 
EDGE-certified warehouses of 18,000 square metres in Mauritius. Compared with conventional warehouse 
developments, the facilities achieved energy savings of 38% to 41%, water savings of up to 47% and reductions 
in embodied carbon of up to 39%. The three warehouses are also expected to avoid more than 36 tonnes of 
operational CO2 emissions annually.

## Sustainable Finance

## Turning glass waste into value

## Alignment with relevant SDGs:

## Birmingham Glass Solutions Ltd

Target 11.6
Target 12.5

Mauritius currently lacks large-scale infrastructure for recycling flat and automotive glass, with significant volumes 
of recoverable material still disposed of in landfill.

MCB Ltd provided a sustainable loan to Birmingham Glass Solutions Ltd to finance specialised glass recycling 
equipment and support the establishment of a dedicated glass recycling unit. The financing enabled the company 
to process up to 100 tonnes of glass waste annually, converting waste glass from the construction, renovation and 
automotive sectors into valuable secondary raw materials and recycled products. By diverting waste from landfill 
and promoting resource recovery, the project supports the development of a more circular economy in Mauritius.

## Scaling rooftop solar for Mauritius’ logistics sector

## Alignment with relevant SDGs:

## Mauritius Freeport Development

Target 9.4
Target 7.2
Target 13.2

As a key player in Mauritius’ logistics and freeport ecosystem, Mauritius Freeport Development (MFD) operates 
strategic warehousing and distribution facilities supporting national and regional trade. To strengthen its energy 
resilience and reduce reliance on conventional grid electricity, MFD embarked on one of the country’s largest 
rooftop solar initiative across its facilities at Mer Rouge, Port Louis Harbour.

MCB provided sustainable financing for the installation of two grid-tied solar photovoltaic plants with a combined 
capacity of 7.0 MWp. Developed under the Central Electricity Board’s Carbon Neutral Industrial Sector (CNIS) 
Renewable Energy Scheme, the project is expected to generate more than 10.7 GWh of renewable electricity 
annually, supporting increased renewable energy capacity and Mauritius’ transition towards a lower-carbon 
energy mix.
