## Sustainable Investment and Capital Market Advisory

In addition to expanding our sustainable finance offering, we are supporting clients as they transition towards more 
sustainable business models. Across the organisation, business units are increasingly integrating sustainable finance into 
solutions for institutional, corporate and high-net-worth clients, helping them progress their sustainability priorities

## Sustainable investment through MCB Private Wealth Management

MCB Private Wealth Management provides tailored investment and wealth management solutions for clients, 
including financial planning, portfolio management and specialised credit solutions.

Following the finalisation of the Private Wealth Management (PWM) Sustainability Framework for 
international market investments last year, sustainable portfolios were launched in November 2025, 
offering clients investment solutions aligned with responsible investing principles. Between January and June 
2026, 4% of new mandates were for sustainable portfolios, reflecting growing client interest in sustainable 
investing. This early uptake further validates the relevance of incorporating sustainability considerations into 
the Bank’s wealth management strategies.

Throughout the year, PWM focused on building internal capabilities and fostering meaningful client 
engagement on sustainability topics. Ongoing education and awareness initiatives were delivered to private 
bankers and relationship managers, equipping them with the knowledge and tools to support informed client 
conversations on sustainable investing. The Sustainability Framework was further communicated to clients 
through PWM’s quarterly video, shared on LinkedIn and screened in MCB lounges. The PWM Sustainability 
Forum was also established to encourage knowledge-sharing and collaboration on sustainability across teams 
and clients.

Looking ahead, PWM plans to deepen client engagement through a dedicated campaign to 
promote awareness of sustainable investment opportunities and encourage broader adoption of 
responsible investment strategies. Development of additional thematic and sustainability-focused 
investment products is also planned from FY27, including products for self-managed clients. 
These initiatives support PWM’s broader aim of helping clients integrate long-term sustainability 
considerations into their investment decisions.

Beyond this, PWM aims to further expand its contribution to positive social impact and community development by donating 
a portion of the management fees generated from sustainable portfolios to selected NGOs. PWM clients and MCB staff will 
also be invited to contribute  to this initiative. This forms part of PWM’s broader ambition to create sustainable value for 
clients, communities and future generations.

## Sustainable finance advisory through MCB Capital Markets

MCB Capital Markets provides a broad range of services to corporate and individual clients, including corporate 
finance advisory, asset management, stockbroking, structured products and registry services. Our team advises 
clients wishing to start or expand their activities in Africa on their strategic financing and investment initiatives.

In line with the Group’s broader sustainable finance agenda, MCB Capital Markets is developing sustainable finance solutions 
for its clients. As part of its advisory services, MCB Capital Markets advises clients on developing and implementing their 
sustainable finance frameworks in line with the ICMA Green Bond Principles and Sustainability-Linked Bond Principles. 
MCB Capital Markets also advises on the structuring and execution of capital market transactions, including the issuance of 
sustainability-linked bonds and green bonds.

78
MCB Group Limited | Sustainability Report 2026


---
*Page 80*


## Sustainable Finance

## FY26 sustainable finance transactions

MCB Capital Markets advised on three domestic sustainable finance transactions this year, totalling Rs 3.6 billion.

These mandates reflect growing interest among Mauritian corporates in using sustainable finance frameworks and labelled 
instruments to raise capital for environmental and social priorities.

## Dyospower Ltd: Combining innovative finance and renewable energy

MCB Capital Markets advised MCB Real Assets on its strategic equity investment in Dyospower Ltd, a platform 
dedicated to financing renewable energy projects in Mauritius and across the region. The vehicle focuses on 
investments in solar, hydro and wind projects. With a strong project pipeline, Dyospower aims to become a 
leading commercial and industrial renewable energy player in the region. This initiative reflects our commitment 
to supporting the energy transition and contributes directly to the  objective of reducing CO2 emissions associated 
with its electricity consumption.

MCB Capital Markets also acted as Sustainable Finance Advisor to Dyospower Ltd on a Rs 200 million green bond, 
supporting the development of a first project of 4.8 MWp solar farm at Poudre d’Or. The facility was commissioned 
in April 2026 and is expected to generate approximately 7,700 MWh of clean electricity annually and avoid around 
6,000 tonnes of CO2 emissions each year.

## ER Group Ltd: Building a framework for sustainable financing

MCB Capital Markets supported ER Group Ltd in structuring and drafting its sustainable finance framework,  
which was launched in June 2026. The framework provides a reference point for ER Group Ltd’s future bond 
issuances and structured financing transactions. It covers both use-of-proceeds instruments, including green 
bonds, and sustainability-linked bonds, and establishes eligible environmental and social financing categories.  
FSD Africa provided technical support alongside MCB Capital Markets, while Moody’s Ratings provided a Second 
Party Opinion.

## Oficea Ltd: Linking financing to greener buildings

MCB Capital Markets acted as Sustainable Finance Advisor to Oficea Ltd on a Rs 3.4 billion sustainability-linked 
bond. The bond is linked to a key performance indicator measuring the percentage of gross leasable area with 
recognised green building certifications, including LEED and EDGE.

## Looking ahead

MCB Capital Markets will continue to support clients across the sustainable finance spectrum, 
from the development of sustainable finance frameworks to the structuring and issuance 
of green and other innovative financing instruments. By combining technical expertise with 
an understanding of markets and investor needs, MCB Capital Markets aims to deepen its 
contribution to the development of sustainable capital markets in Mauritius and across Africa.

## Financing electric and hybrid vehicles

60 electric and hybrid vehicle loans valued at Rs 93 million were disbursed during FY26

Our Green Drive loan scheme encouraged employees to choose electric or hybrid vehicles. Between July and October 2025, 
33 Green Drive loans valued at Rs 52 million were disbursed to employees. In October 2025, the scheme was repriced 
following a review of prevailing market conditions. MCB continued to finance electric and hybrid vehicle purchases for the 
remainder of FY26, with a further 27 loans valued at Rs 41 million disbursed.

## Green Lease

Serving individuals and businesses, our Green Lease product covers the acquisition costs for eligible electric or hybrid 
vehicles9 as well as PV-based charging stations (excluding installation costs) and other sustainable equipment. In FY26, we 
continued to see strong uptake of our Green Lease product, underpinned by consumer demand and increasing availability 
of electric and hybrid vehicles in the local market. The value of Green Lease contracts decreased by 9% from Rs 1,349 million 
in FY25 to Rs 1,232 million, representing approximately 38% of total leases disbursed by MCB Leasing. Eligibility criteria for 
Green Lease were also tightened during the year.

## MCB Maldives

Expanded its sustainable finance offering during 
FY26 with the introduction of a sustainable loan for 
corporate clients. The product provides concessional 
financing for eligible sustainability-related initiatives. 
Employees also participated in a Maldives Monetary 
Authority workshop on the Maldives Sustainable 
Finance Roadmap 2025–2030. The workshop aimed 
to provide a strategic framework for the development 
of sustainable finance in the Maldives, including 
strengthening sustainability-related risk management,

## MCB Madagascar

Continued supporting sustainable development projects 
through the EUR 6 million SUNREF (Sustainable Use of

with Agence Française de Développement. Since its launch in

renewable energy and environmental protection projects,

impacts. Looking ahead, MCB Madagascar plans to launch 
a sustainable loan, which will be closely aligned with the 
Group’s sustainable finance framework and sustainable 
finance line.

## MCB Seychelles

Launched its reviewed Green Loan offering during FY26,

Bank also leveraged its participation in the Seychelles 50th 
Independence Expo to raise awareness of its financing products, 
including the Green Loan, and engage prospective customers.

Financial Inclusion  
and Financial Health

Financial inclusion empowers individuals and 
businesses to use financial services with confidence, 
manage their money well and take part fully in the 
economy. When households are financially resilient 
and enterprises can grow, the benefits reach whole 
communities and strengthen the country’s long-term 
economic prospects.

At MCB Group, we seek to reduce barriers to financial services 
while helping individuals and businesses access the finance, 
banking solutions, knowledge and support they need at different 
stages of their journey. This spans support for entrepreneurs and 
growing businesses, accessible physical and digital banking, and 
initiatives to build financial capability, support better financial 
decision-making and help people manage their finances and build 
resilience over time.

## MCB Microfinance

Micro-entrepreneurs play a vital role in the economy, providing everyday goods and services, such as food vending, repair 
shops and market stalls, which support local production and generate income for families and communities. Yet many 
operate in the space between the informal and formal economy and may not have the collateral, documented cash flows or 
financial history typically required to access conventional bank financing. 
 
MCB Microfinance was created to help bridge this gap. 
 
Since launching in Mauritius in 2016 and extending its operations to Rodrigues in 2017, MCB Microfinance has widened 
access to finance for micro-entrepreneurs, combining appropriate financing with personalised support to help clients make 
informed investment decisions that sustain or grow their businesses.

Over the past decade, this has contributed to greater financial inclusion and economic opportunity for thousands of micro-
entrepreneurs, supporting livelihoods, families and the wider local economy. MCB Microfinance also contributes to the 
Group’s sustainable finance agenda, with financing for eligible micro-enterprises and women-led businesses falling within the 
social financing criteria of the Bank’s sustainable finance framework.

Read more about our sustainable finance framework on page 69.

## Down from 11.9% in FY25

Relationship Officers spend time with micro-entrepreneurs to understand how their businesses operate and their individual 
circumstances. They also assess whether the financing requested is appropriate and manageable within the client’s 
repayment capacity. This close-to-client approach helps reduce the risk of borrowing creating additional financial pressure. 
To help strengthen their day-to-day financial management capabilities, clients also receive basic financial literacy training, 
including introductory guidance on good accounting practices and cash flow management.

Ultimately, MCB Microfinance’s ten-year story is about the people behind each loan and helping 
entrepreneurs move from immediate needs towards greater confidence and longer-term possibility.

## Client spotlight:

## Ulric François, photographer and videographer

When Ulric François approached MCB Microfinance in 2021, Covid-19 had made it difficult 
to secure financing for the equipment he needed to fulfil new contracts. MCB Microfinance 
financed a drone and other equipment, enabling him to invest in his business.

“That first financing gave me the opportunity to start building the business I wanted. I don’t 
think I would have come this far without it. Since then, I have continued investing in new 
equipment and returned to MCB Microfinance for further financing as the business has grown.”

## Aurélie Leclézio, CEO of MCB Microfinance

“MCB Microfinance is a tangible expression of Success Beyond Numbers – creating opportunity 
by making finance more accessible to those who may otherwise be excluded. As we enter our 
next decade, we remain committed to growing alongside micro-entrepreneurs in Mauritius and 
Rodrigues, helping more people build stronger businesses, sustainable livelihoods and a more 
inclusive local economy.”

## Lokal is Beautiful

The Group launched Lokal is Beautiful in 2019, following an economic and sustainability study of the local economy 
conducted by Utopies4. The study highlighted opportunities to strengthen Mauritius’ prosperity by increasing the multiplier 
effect and circulating wealth locally. In response, MCB launched several key initiatives, including our Lokal is Beautiful (LIB) 
loan scheme. We further developed strategic partnerships to support local economic development.

## LIB loan scheme

Through our LIB loan scheme, we provide accessible financing to micro-enterprises, SMEs and mid-market enterprises that 
contribute positively to Mauritius’ economy and environment. The scheme supports businesses at different stages of growth, 
helping them finance working capital needs, investment and expansion.

Eligibility is structured around four areas that reflect opportunities to strengthen local production, circularity, 
innovation and responsible manufacturing:

## Building awareness of Lokal is Beautiful

During FY26, we promoted the LIB loan scheme through targeted radio and digital campaigns aimed at increasing 
awareness among local entrepreneurs, SMEs and business owners. Targeted advertising also helped reach 
audiences actively seeking business loans and financing solutions, supporting the campaign’s objective of 
connecting relevant businesses with the scheme.

## Case study: Strengthening local food production and resilience

Supported by LIB loan financing, O-Formulation Ltd, a local import and trading company, acquired Esprit Vert, Mauritius’ 
largest greenhouse vegetable producer, in 2026. The integration is expected to create synergies between O-Formulation’s 
existing distribution activities (including fertilisers, substrates and agricultural equipment) and Esprit Vert’s local 
greenhouse operations, including:

Direct access to high-quality inputs for 
greenhouse production

Real-time product testing and feedback to 
improve fertiliser and substrate formulations

A more vertically integrated operating model, 
helping to reduce costs and strengthen supply-
chain resilience

More efficient logistics through shared 
warehousing and distribution

By expanding local production capacity, the investment is also expected to support import substitution and food security, 
while strengthening O-Formulation’s position across the agricultural value chain.

## Strategic partnerships

We have developed strategic partnerships with key local players to build a stronger and more enabling entrepreneurial 
environment within Mauritius. These partnerships contribute in different ways to our ambitions for a Maker Island, Circular 
Island and Smart Island, while supporting the growth and visibility of Made in Moris businesses.

We remain committed to making banking inclusive and accessible for everyone. 
 
During the year, we continued to assess how accessibility features for customers with visual impairments could be integrated 
into our ATM network.

We also strengthened our focus on physical accessibility within our branches. At least one People Manager in each branch 
completed Disability Awareness for Better Customer Experience training, aimed at promoting a more accessible and inclusive 
banking experience. Independent physical accessibility audits were completed at three pilot branches to help us identify and 
address physical access barriers.

Read more about the Bridges Programme launched under ERG on page 139.

Building on this work, we are introducing a Disability and Inclusion Handbook to guide our approach across branches and 
ATMs. The handbook reinforces our commitment to ensure that every customer can access and use our services with 
dignity, confidence and as much independence as possible. It sets out our proposed approach to supporting customers with 
different needs, particularly persons with disabilities, by recognising that barriers may arise from environments, systems, 
communication or attitudes. It therefore calls on us to be proactive, flexible and attentive in removing these barriers, while 
upholding core principles of fairness, privacy, safety and respect.

For business customers, dedicated SME Hubs provide additional physical access to banking services and support.

## Financial Inclusion and Financial Health

## Expanding digital access

Digital channels continue to broaden the ways in which customers can bank and transact. Through our innovative 
digital and self-service solutions, we empower customers with access to banking services, anywhere and anytime.

During FY26, we expanded access to secure 
digital payments through the launch of 
Mastercard Tap-to-Pay in November 2025 
and Apple Pay in May 2026. These solutions 
provide customers with a faster, safer 
and more convenient way to transact in 
Mauritius and abroad, enabling seamless 
contactless payments without the need to 
carry physical cards or cash.

We enhanced MCB Juice, our flagship retail mobile banking application, focused on strengthening platform stability 
and resilience while making recent transaction information easier to access.  
 
We continued to offer business customers support through digital platforms, including MCB JuicePro and Internet Banking 
Pro, which provide real-time visibility over accounts, payments, cashflows and business operations, enabling customers to 
make more informed financial decisions and strengthen financial discipline.

We continued to simplify selected customer journeys and expand self-service options.  
 
Straight-Through Processing for prepaid card applications replaced a previously paper-based process, reducing turnaround 
time from four days to two days, while enhancements to IB Pro enabled selected trade finance customers to submit import 
loan applications and manage loan rescheduling digitally. These developments give customers greater choice in how they 
access and manage banking services, while reducing reliance on manual and branch-based processes.

We further expanded our self-service banking capabilities through the deployment of more than 50 InstaBank kiosks across 
our branch footprint in Mauritius. Offering secure card-and-PIN access to a broad range of banking services, including 
account management, payments, transfers, bill payments and mobile top-ups, InstaBank is accelerating the migration of 
various transactions to digital channels while reducing reliance on manual branch processing.

Read more about how we are simplifying selected customer journeys in our 2026 MCB Group Annual Report.

We remain committed to helping customers navigate digital channels and build greater confidence in managing 
their banking independently.  
 
Recognising that access to digital solutions does not automatically translate into the confidence to use them, we are 
introducing Digital Ambassadors across our branch network to provide hands-on assistance to customers who may be less 
familiar with digital banking.

We continued to provide remote support through our Contact Centre via WhatsApp and Facebook Messenger, helping 
customers access assistance through their preferred channels. During FY26, the Contact Centre handled approximately 1 
million customer interactions. We also commenced a multi-year programme to improve first-contact resolution and simplify 
customer interactions, while ensuring customers can continue to access human support when needed.

## Supporting access for younger customers

We continued to offer Pack 18.25, designed to provide young adults aged 18 to 25 with a simple and accessible entry point to 
banking. The pack combines a savings account, debit card and access to online and mobile banking.

We complement access to banking with financial education aimed at helping customers and communities make 
more informed decisions, plan for the future and navigate financial and digital risks. Our financial capability efforts 
focus on households, rural communities, older customers and people who may otherwise face financial or digital 
exclusion, combining face-to-face engagement with digital learning tools and resources.

informed day-to-day money management, longer-term planning and greater 
awareness of financial risks.

During the year, MCB Madagascar took steps to make banking more accessible and build financial capability.

MCB Madagascar continued to promote greater awareness and use of electronic payments during FY26, supporting

## Supporting Africa’s energy security and economic development

## Africa’s energy landscape2

Africa’s energy and infrastructure needs are complex and significant.

The continent contributes less than 4%1 of global CO2 emissions, and its emissions per person remain well below the 
global average, yet it is highly vulnerable to climate change. At the same time, many African economies remain exposed to 
carbonintense sectors given their substantial contribution to the continent’s revenue generation and energy supply. Many 
countries also face challenges in energy access, affordability and reliability while needing to invest in resilient infrastructure, 
renewable energy and the value chains that support the global energy transition. Financial institutions must therefore 
proactively manage their exposure to carbon-intensive sectors while facilitating energy transitions that are economically 
sustainable and socially inclusive.

## CO2 emissions per capita

CO2 emissions from burning fossil fuels and industrial processes. This includes emissions from transport, electricity 
generation and heating, but not land-use change. generation and heating, but not land-use change.

## Supporting Africa’s energy security and economic development

## Our approach

In line with Vision 2030, we aim to advance sustainable development for communities and business across the 
continent by financing essential sectors that are critical for energy security and renewable energy deployment.

To achieve our strategic objectives, we reorganised our Corporate and Institutional Banking segment in FY25 through the 
establishment of Global Coverage and Global Structured Finance teams. Within Global Structured Finance, our Energy, 
Infrastructure and Commodities (EIC) business offers lending solutions to a wide range of asset classes within key target 
sectors. EIC is organised into three dedicated teams to strengthen operational focus, efficiency and risk control, namely 
Natural Resources Finance, Power & Infrastructure and Commodity Trade Finance.

The Natural Resources Finance and Power & Infrastructure teams focus on medium-term structured financing opportunities 
within their respective sectors, while Commodity Trade Finance provides shorter-term trade finance and risk-cover solutions 
across relevant energy and natural resources value chains.

Together, these capabilities enable MCB to support a broader range of financing needs across selected African markets, 
helping facilitate investment and trade in sectors that underpin economic activity, energy security and long-term 
development.

## Our sector positioning

In 2022, the Bank stopped all new cross-border financing of coal infrastructure and trade, whether thermal or 
metallurgical. Locally, the Bank stopped financing new coal-fired power plants.

## How we assess EIC financing

We recognise that our target sectors may carry environmental, social and governance-related risk that must be 
carefully managed. 
 
All EIC financing, including commodity trade finance, is assessed through MCB’s established credit and risk management 
processes. The Bank’s E&S Risk Policy and Credit Risk Policy provide the overarching framework for assessing E&S and credit 
risks, including exclusions, due diligence, sanctioning, contractual requirements and ongoing monitoring.

During FY26, MCB further introduced dedicated sector risk policies for Oil and Gas and Metals and Mining. These policies 
strengthen how the Bank’s broader credit and E&S risk management requirements are applied in sectors with heightened 
E&S risks.

The Bank’s ESRM process further requires that high E&S risk projects undergo a climate change risk assessment (CCRA) to 
identify associated physical and transition risks and corresponding mitigation measures. Furthermore, the Bank is integrating 
climate risk into its risk management framework, which will encompass portfolio-level financial risk management as well as 
E&S risk management.

Read more about our approach to ESRM from page 59 and Climate Risk from page 64.

Our E&S Risk Policy is available here.

103
MCB Group Limited | Sustainability Report 2026

*Page 105*


## Our sector focus

## Oil and gas

Oil and gas remain central to many African economies and continue to provide a significant share of energy supply. Africa 
accounted for around 7% of global oil output in 2023 and approximately 40% of natural gas discovered worldwide between 
2010 and 2020.3 We believe that Africa must leverage these energy sources to meet its growth and development objectives, 
while ensuring the affordability and availability of energy supplies.

Given the prominent role that fossil fuels continue to play in many African markets, MCB will continue to support selected oil 
financing to meet the continent’s immediate energy needs. Building on existing relationships and sector experience, the Bank 
is also seeking to increase its participation in selected opportunities across the gas value chain, recognising the short- to 
medium-term transition role that natural gas plays in supporting the continent’s energy transition.

The Bank considers financing related to upstream, midstream and downstream oil and gas activities.

We acknowledge the climate and E&S risks that may arise from continued reliance on oil and gas, including methane 
emissions, carbon lock-in and stranded asset exposure as global energy systems transition, and we are committed to 
managing these risks.

E&S due diligence
What we will not finance

## MCB does not finance oil prospection.4

The Bank’s Oil and Gas Sector Risk Policy covers oil 
and gas trading, storage, production and processing 
(including oil refineries, liquefaction and natural 
gas export or regasification terminals). Short-term 
commodity-trading exposure is subject to minimum  
E&S requirements, including legal-compliance clauses. 
 
Our Oil and Gas Sector Risk Policy is available here.

Oil and gas transactions are screened against MCB’s 
E&S exclusion requirements.

While MCB is not yet in a position to report financed emissions, oil and gas exposures are likely to be material to future 
assessments. To advance this work, MCB has begun applying the PCAF methodology to selected areas of its lending portfolio, 
including the methodologies, data inputs and governance arrangements required to support assessment. As MCB’s climate-
risk and data capabilities mature, the Bank is committed to strengthening its assessment and measurement of emissions-
related risks in these sectors.

## Metals and minerals

As important inputs into renewable energy, storage and electrification technologies, the global energy transition is driving 
demand for critical metals and minerals found in abundance in Africa. It is estimated that the continent holds approximately 
30% of the world’s critical mineral reserves, including 40% of the world’s gold, 70% of the world’s cobalt and 75% of the 
world’s platinum and chromium.5

To unlock this potential, investment is required in responsible mineral processing, refining and value addition. However, 
mining and minerals-related activities can carry significant E&S risks, including impacts on natural habitats, water supply, 
biodiversity, greenhouse gas emissions, health and safety, working conditions and indigenous peoples’ rights.

Building on our experience in financing complex transactions and providing tailored financing to higher-risk sectors, 
including oil and gas, we are extending these disciplines to metals and minerals to ensure relevant activities are financed 
responsibly.

3Sources: International Energy Agency, Statista.

4Oil prospection refers to the search for new oil resources, including drilling in areas where no oil formations have yet been identified but where geological studies suggest 
hydrocarbons may be present. It differs from the oil exploration and production financing shown in the EIC financing exposure table (page **), which relates only to fields 
already in production.

5Sources: World Bank, International Energy Agency.

104
MCB Group Limited | Sustainability Report 2026

*Page 106*


## Supporting Africa’s energy security and economic development

Our focus is on financing selected opportunities that support the extraction and trading of selected metals and minerals, 
working with well-established miners and traders with high environmental, social and governance standards. As the business 
develops, the Bank is progressively strengthening its internal sector expertise and capabilities while monitoring market 
developments and emerging risks and opportunities.

E&S due diligence
What we will not finance

The Bank’s Metals and Mining Sector Risk Policy requires 
enhanced E&S due diligence on all metals and mining 
financing, covering production, trading activities, 
transportation, storage and export infrastructure and 
processing such as smelting and refining.   
 
Our Metals and Mining Sector Risk Policy is available 
here.

Metals and mining transactions are screened 
against MCB’s E&S exclusion requirements, including 
restrictions relating to activities involving forced or 
child labour, illegal activities and destruction of critical 
habitat, which are particularly pertinent to the metals 
and mining sector.

## Power and Infrastructure

Power and infrastructure are key enablers for economic growth in Africa. While there have been some improvements 
in Africa’s Infrastructure Development Index, significant investment is still needed in power, water and transport. More 
than 600 million people lack access to electricity, around 53% of roads are unpaved (isolating regions from economic 
opportunities) and 65% of Africa’s population lack access to basic sanitation.6

MCB aims to support selected opportunities in renewable energy alongside broader infrastructure financing that contributes 
to socio-economic development, including power generation and infrastructure, such as water, transport, ports and other 
energy-related assets.

Building on its experience in structuring complex financing solutions, MCB seeks to work with experienced project sponsors 
and infrastructure players across selected African markets. MCB continues to explore financing structures that can help 
mobilise capital into renewable energy and infrastructure projects, including green financing solutions such as green bonds, 
where appropriate.

Power and infrastructure transactions are assessed through MCB’s credit and E&S risk-management processes, with 
the Equator Principles and IFC Performance Standards applied, where relevant, based on the nature and risk profile of 
the financing.

Case study: Powering Africa’s energy transition by enabling renewable 
baseload power for energy-intensive industries  
 
In November 2025, MCB participated as a principal lender, contributing USD  
50 million in the second tranche of a USD 200 million portfolio financing facility for 
Cross Boundary Energy (CBE), a leading pan-African renewable energy developer 
serving commercial and industrial clients. The facility was arranged by Standard 
Bank South Africa alongside a consortium of regional and international lenders.

The financing supports CBE’s rollout of renewable energy infrastructure across 
Africa. This includes the construction of CBE’s flagship Kamoa-Kakula Solar PV 
and Battery Energy Storage System Baseload Project in the Democratic Republic 
of Congo – a first-of-its-kind hybrid system combining 222 MW of solar capacity 
and 526 MWh of battery storage to deliver 30 MW of reliable, baseload renewable 
power to Kamoa Copper S.A., Africa’s largest copper mine.

By enabling continuous, dispatchable renewable power for a hard-to-abate, 
energy-intensive sector, this transaction illustrates how MCB can support the 
decarbonisation of industrial demand rather than confining investment to 
standalone generation assets.

The portfolio-financing structure, with its built-in accordion feature for future 
upsizing, is designed to accelerate replication of similar renewable-baseload 
solutions for other commercial and industrial clients across the continent, 
reinforcing our long-standing commitment to supporting sustainable energy 
infrastructure and expanding access to modern energy solutions.

6Sources: World Bank Development Indicators, African Development Bank, Global Energy Monitoring.

## Looking Ahead

As Africa navigates its energy transition, MCB remains committed to financing the continent’s 
immediate energy needs while progressively expanding its presence across Gas, Power & 
Infrastructure, and Metals & Minerals to support a more sustainable and resilient future.

As climate science, regulatory expectations and market practice continue to evolve, MCB will continue 
reviewing how it assesses carbon-intensive exposures and transition-related opportunities, including 
the metrics and portfolio information needed to support decision-making and disclosure.

Livelihoods  
Carbon Funds: 
Building resilience 
at scale

In line with Vision 2030 and our 
broader ambition to support Africa’s 
sustainable growth and lower-carbon 
development, MCB Group invests in 
the Livelihoods Carbon Funds (LCF).

These funds finance large-scale, long-term nature-
based projects that restore ecosystems, strengthen 
rural livelihoods and build resilience across Africa 
and other developing regions. Building on our 
participation in LCF3, we deepened this commitment 
in FY26 by joining LCF4 as a corporate investor.

Our investment in these funds provides future 
access to high-integrity carbon credits. As our climate 
approach and reporting evolve, we will disclose the 
role of carbon credits in relation to our scope 1 and 
scope 2 and selected scope 3 emissions, excluding 
financed emissions, including the extent of their use 
and the nature and verification of the credits, with 
reference to IFRS S2 disclosure requirements.

## Project spotlight: Supporting more resilient cocoa farming in Ghana

One of the projects funded by LCF3 is the Agro Eco Cocoa Agroforestry Project in Ghana, which aims to increase 
climate resilience while boosting sustainable small-scale cocoa production that benefits farming households.

Cocoa farming is an important source of livelihoods across West Africa. However, climate change, soil degradation and 
declining ecosystem health have implications for agricultural productivity and the livelihoods of farming communities. 
Agroforestry offers one way to build greater resilience by integrating trees and other vegetation into cocoa farms, helping to 
improve soil health, support biodiversity and create more resilient farming systems.

Through LCF3, the Agro Eco Cocoa Agroforestry Project aims to strengthen the climate resilience of cocoa production while 
supporting farming households and enhancing the ecosystem services on which their livelihoods depend.

Environmental 
Footprint

We monitor and manage the direct 
environmental impacts arising from our 
day-to-day operations, with a focus on GHG 
emissions, energy and water consumption, 
paper use and waste generation.

While these impacts are significantly lower than the 
indirect environmental impacts associated with our 
financing activities, we remain committed to managing our 
own operational impact responsibly. Our approach focuses 
on tracking consumption and performance, identifying 
opportunities to improve resource efficiency and reduce 
waste and integrating practical sustainable practices 
across our operations, including measures to address food 
waste. We also continue to build employee sustainability 
awareness and action across our workforce.  
 
Unless otherwise stated, operational environmental 
performance data in this chapter relates to the 2025 
calendar year. This differs from the Group’s FY26 
financial reporting period.

GHG emissions 
 
We continue to monitor and manage the GHG emissions 
associated with our own operations. 
 
Our approach combines the purchase of carbon credits that 
support carbon avoidance and/or removal projects equivalent 
to our operational emissions (scope 1, scope 2* and selected 
scope 3 emissions, excluding financed emissions). These 
complement our efforts to manage resource use and reduce 
emissions within our own operations.

*Scope 2 emissions were previously addressed through International Renewable Energy 
Certificates (I-RECs), most recently for our 2022 grid electricity consumption. Following 
the expiry of the I-REC contract, scope 2 is now included in our carbon credit purchases.

## Environmental Footprint

Performance 
 
In 2025, total GHG emissions amounted to 18,966 tCO2e, representing a 12% year-on-year increase (2024: 16,928 tCO2e) 
and a cumulative increase of 3% against our 2018 baseline of 18,411 tCO2e. This is the first year that total emissions have 
exceeded the baseline since it was established. The increase was driven primarily by higher scope 2 and scope 3 emissions.

Purchased electricity (scope 2) nonetheless remains the main source of emissions within our operational footprint. Scope 
2 emissions stood at a total of 12,403 tCO2e, reflecting a 4.4% increase in grid electricity consumption. Scope 3 emissions 
(excluding upstream categories and downstream financing activities) rose by 19.6%, mainly due to a 42.7% increase in 
business travel to 2,627 tCO2e. This was partly offset by a 7% reduction in scope 1 emissions to 398 tCO2e, reflecting lower 
generator and vehicle use.

## 398

fluorinated gas losses and fuel for company vehicles

Scope 3: For MCB, this includes upstream emissions from 
employee commuting and business travel. It excludes other 
upstream categories and downstream financing activities.

MCB’s GHG emissions are calculated by a third-party in accordance with the Greenhouse Gas Protocol carbon accounting 
methodology, developed by the World Resources Institute and the World Business Council for Sustainable Development.

## Carbon Credits

In FY25, we purchased carbon credits to compensate for our calendar year 2023 scope 1, scope 2 and partial scope 3 
emissions, representing 16,834 tCO2e. Carbon credits were sourced from two renewable energy projects in Morocco: the 
Khalladi Wind Farm and the Noor Ouarzazate Concentrated Solar Power (CSP) complex.

The Khalladi Wind Farm is located in the Fahs Anjra Province near Tangier. Its 40 turbines have a total capacity of 120 MW 
and feed an average of around 296,100 MWh of clean electricity into the national grid each year, avoiding approximately 
71,000 tCO2e annually. The project also funds school transport for more than 480 students, a mobile medical van, two milk 
collection points, support for women’s cooperatives and new roads and wells.

Noor Ouarzazate is situated southeast of Marrakesh and is the world’s largest CSP project. It adds an average of 699 GWh 
of renewable electricity to the grid each year, avoiding approximately 400,000 tCO2e annually. Beyond its energy output, the 
project has financed improved medical care, female empowerment initiatives, support for agricultural development, access 
to education and sports activities as well as infrastructure improvements.

## Building our capacity to measure financed emissions

This year, we made progress on our journey to understand the impact of our financing activities along our value chain. 
 
Financed emissions associated with our financing activities form part of our broader climate impact. Measuring these 
emissions is therefore important to understanding climate-related exposure across our portfolio. However, it depends on 
consistent methodologies and reliable data.

2025
2026

In FY25, MCB Ltd embarked on a structured 
journey to strengthen its climate accountability 
and align its practices with international 
standards. The Bank joined the Partnership 
for Carbon Accounting Financials (PCAF), a 
global, industry-led initiative that provides 
a harmonised approach to measuring and 
disclosing financed emissions.

In FY26, we launched a pilot to develop a 
practical understanding of the requirements 
for applying the PCAF methodology. The pilot 
assessed data availability and methodological 
requirements and identified the stakeholders, 
roles and governance processes needed to 
support future calculation and disclosure.

## Key outcomes achieved during FY26

Cross-functional 
collaboration

Building  
capability

Data quality  
insights

The pilot also highlighted the 
importance of strengthening 
emissions-related data collection. 
Borrower-level GHG emissions 
data was often limited, incomplete 
or unavailable, requiring the 
use of sector-average emission 
factors and proxy data in line 
with the PCAF methodology. 
As PCAF’s data-quality scoring 
makes these limitations visible, 
this exercise provided valuable 
insights into where improvements 
in data governance and client 
engagement will be required.

The pilot brought together 
teams from Sustainability, Credit 
Risk and Credit Monitoring, 
alongside engagement with 
our Coverage team. This cross-
functional collaboration helped 
clarify responsibilities for 
data collection, validation and 
methodology application.

Capacity building was another 
key focus area. Stakeholder 
engagement sessions reached 
approximately 40 colleagues, 
covering financed emissions, 
attribution principles and data 
quality requirements, supported 
by peer learning through PCAF 
quarterly calls.

## Looking Ahead

The pilot has provided a structured roadmap for refining our measurement of financed 
emissions and expanding portfolio coverage. As this work develops, the resulting insights will 
support more informed climate risk assessment, client engagement and sustainable finance 
decision-making.

Find out more about PCAF’s Global GHG Accounting and Reporting Standard here.

## Environmental Footprint

## Energy intensity

We aim to reduce energy intensity by investing in energy-efficient solutions and considering energy performance 
when selecting new equipment. We are also exploring opportunities to increase the contribution of renewable 
energy to our energy mix.

## Performance

Energy intensity fell from 3,400 kWh per FTE in 2024 to 3,202 kWh per FTE in 2025, a 5.8% reduction and a 30.7% reduction 
against the 2018 baseline of 4,618 kWh per FTE. This is mainly due to improved process efficiency.

The solar farm at our office at St Jean generated 448 MWh of electricity in 2025, down from 498 MWh in 2024. The decrease 
mainly reflected adverse weather conditions and maintenance activities.

In addition, we continued to implement medium-scale distributed generation photovoltaic (PV) projects across our 
operations, with 25 kW PV systems installed at five branch locations. Four of these systems were connected to the grid during 
the year, with commissioning of the fifth under way. These installations are intended to reduce reliance on grid electricity and 
increase the contribution of renewable energy generated across our operations.

## Water intensity

As Mauritius is a water-stressed island, we focus on monitoring and reducing our water consumption and 
implementing water-saving projects throughout our operations.

Performance 
 
Water intensity fell from 11.6 m³ per FTE in 2024 to 8.3 m³ per FTE, a 28.6% reduction and the lowest level recorded since 
monitoring began. Total freshwater consumption decreased by 22.6% to 33,734 m³ (2024: 43,611 m³), taking water intensity 
36.9% below the 2018 baseline of 13.1 m³ per FTE. The reduction mainly reflects process improvements and water-saving 
measures introduced in response to drought conditions in Mauritius, including the suspension of irrigation and certain 
external cleaning activities.

## Paper consumption

We aim to reduce paper consumption by embracing digital solutions and more efficient processes, helping to lower 
our environmental footprint while improving convenience for our customers and employees.

Performance 
 
We classify paper consumption into two categories: paper used in our direct operations, and other paper use associated with 
customer materials and the production of our annual reports. 
 
Total paper consumption decreased by 28.5% from 302.2 tonnes in 2024 to 216.2 tonnes in 2025. The reduction in other 
paper consumption from 96.5 tonnes in 2024 to 39.2 tonnes partly reflects lower purchases of thermal paper rolls in 2025, 
following higher stock levels carried forward from the previous year.  
 
We continued to encourage customers to subscribe to e-statements alongside encouraging shareholders to adopt electronic 
versions of our annual reports.

## Environmental Footprint

## Reducing paper consumption through digitalisation

During FY26, we continued to shift customer journeys and internal processes towards digital channels, including

online customer onboarding and eKYC. This has enabled us to eliminate up to 80% of paper-based processes, with 
paper now largely retained only where physical signatures are still required. We also continued to simplify and 
digitise supporting documentation and selected card and transaction processes to reduce unnecessary paper use.

Looking ahead, we will continue reviewing operational processes and expanding digital functionality to further 
reduce reliance on paper while improving efficiency and customer convenience.

## Waste segregation and recycling

We continue to reduce waste, increase recycling and support more responsible resource use across our operations. 
 
Our waste segregation and management system supports the collection and recycling of a range of waste streams, including 
plastic, alkaline batteries, coffee capsules, printer ink toners and e-waste. Employees are also encouraged to bring selected 
household waste to designated collection points at MCB’s buildings for recycling. We work with registered waste and 
recycling service providers to ensure appropriate collection, treatment and disposal.

## Food waste

We are committed to reducing food waste across our operations. 
 
In 2021, we joined the Food Waste Prevention Project in Mauritius, a nine-month initiative aimed at reducing food waste and 
its associated environmental impacts. Through the programme, the two restaurants at our Port-Louis head office and St Jean 
office achieved Gold certification under The PLEDGE™ on Food Waste. We have since retained this certification and continue 
to focus on reducing food waste across our operations.

Performance 
 
In 2025, we generated 11.4 tonnes of food waste (26.18 grams per serving), a substantial decrease of 23% from 14.9 tonnes 
(74 grams per serving) in 2024. The decrease in food waste reflects continued efforts to monitor, manage and optimise food 
production and consumption, supported by sustainable purchasing and portion control measures.

We continued our animal-feed initiative throughout 2025, diverting suitable kitchen scraps, mainly vegetable peelings, to 
a nearby local farm for use as animal feed and helping reduce organic waste sent to landfill. We also continued to conduct 
evening sales for unsold meals of the day to employees, preventing good food from going to waste.

Raising awareness regarding food waste among employees remained an area of focus, supported by campaigns and 
initiatives that promote a ‘zero-food waste’ mindset. We also worked closely with staff to identify opportunities to improve 
efficiencies across both our restaurants, including optimising portion sizes and refining menus to ensure they remain 
relevant and aligned with employee needs and preferences.

## Sustainability awareness and engagement

We engage employees on our corporate sustainability programme and purpose, Success Beyond Numbers, through 
targeted awareness and engagement initiatives.  
 
Facilitated by the Central Sustainability Office, these sessions build understanding of MCB’s sustainability journey and key 
environmental and social priorities, while creating opportunities for employees to discuss how sustainability can be further 
embedded across the business.

Employee induction  
 
Sustainability forms part of our employee induction programme, introducing new recruits to our sustainability approach and 
key topics, including climate change and social inclusion. Interactive discussions help to cultivate sustainability awareness 
while encouraging new employees to act as sustainability ambassadors.

Sustainability townhalls  
 
We also extend sustainability awareness across the wider workforce through SBU townhalls. During FY26, the Central 
Sustainability Office facilitated seven sustainability townhalls to further enhance employee awareness of our purpose, 
corporate sustainability programme and key sustainability milestones.

## Targeted environmental awareness workshops

During FY26, we held one Biodiversity Fresk and two Ocean Fresk workshops to deepen employees’ understanding of 
biodiversity and ocean-related challenges. Through collaborative activities and group discussions, the workshops explored 
the interconnections between human activity and environmental pressure, while encouraging participants to consider 
potential solutions and actions.

36 employees participated, from the CSO, SRD and Communications teams,  
as well as Relationship Managers.

## Green Building Awareness Session

Around 20 members of the MCB Coverage team attended a green building awareness session covering design 
considerations, green building certifications, market practices and international standards.

*Page 123*


Building climate finance knowledge with DEG 
 
As climate policies and carbon markets evolve, MCB needs the knowledge to assess new financing opportunities, 
understand the associated risks and support clients as they respond to the transition. To build that understanding 
across the Bank, MCB hosted a three-day workshop with its long-standing development finance partner DEG in 
March 2026, followed by a dedicated leadership session. 
 
Led by DEG the workshop brought together colleagues from key business lines and functions, including banking, financing, 
sustainability, strategy, risk, compliance and internal audit, among others. Together, these teams explored developments in 
climate finance and carbon markets and considered what these could mean for financial institutions. Members of the Bank’s 
leadership team further attended a dedicated leadership session during which key insights from the workshop were shared. 
Discussions were also held on how MCB can position itself in this evolving landscape.

## attended the workshop, representing business

## attended the dedicated leadership session

## lines and key functions across the value chain

The exchange took place as MCB deepened its work with development finance partners. In April 2026, MCB announced a 
USD 100 million, 10-year climate financing facility arranged by Proparco alongside DEG and FMO to support eligible climate 
mitigation and adaptation projects across Africa.

Read more about this climate financing facility in our 2026 MCB Group Annual Report.

Deepening our understanding of Just Transition with UNEP FI 
 
This year, the United Nations Environment Programme Finance Initiative (UNEP FI) facilitated a seminar on Just 
Transition, an evolving concept that emphasises the importance of protecting vulnerable populations while 
maximising the social and economic opportunities underlying the transition to a low-carbon economy.  
 
The seminar was attended by colleagues from key business lines and functions, alongside leadership and Directors. It 
explored the interconnections between climate action, economic development and social inclusion, as well as the role of 
financial institutions in supporting an equitable and inclusive transition.

The seminar included practical case studies from the financial services industry alongside insights into Africa’s climate and 
development priorities, encouraging participants to reflect on how the key principles of Just Transition could inform decisions 
across their roles and functions as the Group evolves its sustainable financing approach.

## Environmental Footprint

Strengthening sustainability reporting with Incite 
 
As sustainability disclosure requirements evolve, we aim to strengthen our understanding of how these 
developments impact our strategy, reporting and engagement activities. In April 2026, advisory partner Incite 
facilitated a two-day capacity-building workshop to strengthen this understanding. 
 
The workshop brought together 25 colleagues from sustainability, strategy, investor relations, internal audit, corporate 
communication, climate risk, finance and legal. Together, these teams reviewed our recent disclosures against the IFRS 
Sustainability Disclosure Standards and GRI Standards, as well as peer practice. Participants reflected on MCB’s material 
sustainability-related impacts, risks and opportunities and how these should inform disclosure. Dedicated sessions with the 
technical reporting team further explored the implications for our reporting process, helping establish a common basis for 
clearer, more consistent disclosure in the years ahead.

Employee donation drive 
 
During FY26, we encouraged employees to support responsible reuse while contributing to communities in need 
through an employee donation drive.  
 
Collection points across several MCB locations enabled employees to donate clothing, shoes, toys, books, accessories and 
other household items in good condition. Conducted in partnership with The Good Shop, donated items were then sorted 
and assessed for reuse. Suitable items were sold through The Good Shop’s retail outlets to support its social mission. Other 
usable items were redistributed to NGOs and community organisations, ensuring that donated goods benefitted vulnerable 
individuals and families while promoting circular economy principles and waste reduction.

Encouraging our employees to reduce, reuse and recycle, in partnership with Mission Verte 
 
Mission Verte is a Mauritian non-profit organisation promoting environmental preservation through education and practical 
action. It encourages people to reduce, reuse, recycle and compost waste, and provides more than 80 collection points 
for recyclables across the country. Since 2022, we have partnered with Mission Verte, supporting its waste collection, 
administrative and awareness activities. We also sponsor a permanent recycling container at MCB St Jean, giving employees a 
convenient way to recycle and encouraging them to put responsible waste management into everyday practice.

## Actions taken by our banking operations in our other Home Markets

During the year, our overseas banking subsidiaries continued to take action to understand and reduce their operational 
environmental impacts.

## MCB Madagascar

Continued to strengthen the measurement and management 
of its operational environmental footprint. Following its carbon 
footprint assessment in 2023, which covered the Analamanga 
Province only , MCB Madagascar extended the scope of its 
2024 assessment to  branches across six regions. Total GHG 
emissions for 2024 amounted to  
544 tCO2e, comprising scope 1 emissions of 151.3 tCO2e,  
scope 2 emissions of 241.8 tCO2e and Scope 3 emissions of 
150.9 tCO2e. Scope 3 covers consumables, upstream energy-
related emissions and business travel. Purchased electricity 
remained the main source of emissions.

Other ongoing operational initiatives included the continued 
digitalisation of brochures to reduce paper use, ongoing 
access to filtered drinking water to reduce reliance on plastic 
packaging and strengthened waste segregation, including 
improved collection of paper and cardboard waste. Building on 
a Climate Fresk workshop hosted last year, MCB Madagascar 
conducted a second workshop in FY26 to strengthen employee 
understanding of climate-related issues.

## MCB Seychelles

Continued to invest in and explore initiatives to reduce 
its operational environmental impact, focusing on 
digitalisation, resource efficiency and waste reduction.

MCB Seychelles digitalised operational processes and 
archives to reduce paper use and physical storage 
requirements, while continuing to introduce energy 
efficient LED and motion sensor lighting. They also 
began transitioning their vehicle fleet towards lower 
emission alternatives, including hybrid and electric 
vehicles. Waste reduction initiatives included repairing 
and reusing eligible equipment, repurposing older 
devices, recycling used printer cartridges and providing 
shredded paper to local businesses and community 
initiatives for reuse. Dedicated coffee capsule recycling 
bins were introduced in cafeterias.

Employee environmental awareness was supported 
through a ‘Small Habits, Big Impacts’ campaign, which 
shared practical guidance on waste reduction, energy 
conservation and responsible resource use.

## Our talent pipeline development strategy

At MCB, we have a clear talent pipeline development strategy. 
 
Our MCB Career Architecture provides a consistent and transparent framework for employee development and career 
progression across the Group, helping us attract and retain business critical talent. Our Career Architecture:

•	
Drives strategy execution by standardising roles and competencies, enabling agile workforce planning and cross-
functional collaboration.

•	
Identifies gaps in roles and capabilities, enabling targeted action to build the skills and leadership pipelines needed to 
execute our growth ambitions.

•	
Offers transparent, structured career progression, reinforcing our commitment to fair opportunity and rewards based 
on contribution and impact.

Guided by our Career Architecture, we have a robust and integrated talent management framework to attract, develop and 
retain high-potential employees. Our talent management framework focuses on:

## Strengthening our Employer Brand

In June 2026, we launched our new Employer Brand, Beyond. Our Employer Brand strengthens our strategic 
approach to talent attraction by clearly articulating what MCB stands for as an employer and what distinguishes the 
experience of working with us. It provides a consistent proposition through which we can engage the capabilities 
and talent needed to support the Group’s ambition. 
 
Grounded in the experiences and perspectives of our people, it provides a shared language for what distinguishes  
MCB Group, fostering collective pride and helping our teams work together as the organisation grow across roles, business 
units and markets. Importantly, it reflects our belief that success extends beyond financial results to the impact our people 
create for our customers and the communities we serve.

 By combining industry expertise with academic excellence, these partnerships aim to foster dynamic learning environments 
that prepare students for the modern workplace, helping to strengthen the pipeline of work-ready graduates and potential

Who these partnerships support: Students

This programme provides hands-on exposure across our Financial Markets division, enhancing employment readiness and 
supporting the development of a sustainable talent pipeline for the financial markets sector.

Who this programme supports: Young, high-potential graduates

•	
Participants followed a structured learning journey combining technical and professional skills training, mentorship and 
coaching by industry professionals, exposure to financial markets operations, industry engagement and networking and

## Beyond Graduate Programme

exposure opportunities across different career pathways within MCB Group.

Six graduates recruited (three male and three female).
•	
90 graduates supported since launch.

•	
We expanded the programme pathways and established partnerships with key market players, further enhancing 
learning opportunities, industry exposure and career development outcomes for participants.

## Promoting lifelong learning for employees

## Evolving our approach to training and development

Technological advancements and evolving market dynamics have reshaped workplaces globally, underlining the importance 
of ensuring our people have the capabilities and competencies they need for the future. To support our growth ambitions 
beyond Mauritius, we further need to ensure that our learning model supports employees across different markets and 
working environments, offering them seamless access to development opportunities.

We are therefore moving from a traditional training model towards a more continuous, practical and employee-led learning 
approach, encouraging colleagues to take greater ownership of their development and integrate learning more closely into 
their day-to-day work.

## Programme spotlight: Strengthening psychological safety

During our annual employee engagement survey, employees identified psychological safety as an area for continued focus. 
In response, psychological safety was incorporated into our employee engagement action plan, and we delivered targeted 
training, awareness sessions, group coaching and workshops to over 1000 employees during the year aimed at fostering 
open dialogue and creating inclusive environments where employees feel respected, heard and confident to speak up.

## Developing Leadership Capabilities at Every Level

## Succession planning

Recruitment
Succession

We identify critical roles and develop a strong internal 
pipeline of potential successors. This is done through 
regular talent reviews, readiness assessments, and 
targeted development plans. We focus on creating 
depth in leadership roles while mitigating key-person 
risks, ensuring business continuity and long-term 
sustainability.

We follow a competency-driven recruitment process 
that aligns with our business needs and culture, 
including assessing alignment with our Values and 
Shared Ways of Working. This includes leveraging 
diverse sourcing channels, structured assessments and 
data-driven decision-making to select top talent.

Both processes are integrated within our talent management framework, ensuring that external hires and internal talent are 
aligned with our organisational priorities and long-term growth.

During FY26, we implemented a Succession Readiness Index and incorporated it into our organisational 
scorecard, strengthening our data-driven approach to succession planning. While progress is being made, initial 
findings indicate that further focus is required to deepen our internal talent pipeline for critical roles. In response, 
we are prioritising targeted development and succession initiatives to improve readiness and build a more 
sustainable leadership bench.

## Leadership in practice: in conversation with employees

“IMPACT Excellence took me in new directions, particularly around the new AI world and how we can apply it in our  
day-to-day work. One of my biggest learnings was to take time to step back rather than simply doing things as we 
always have. I also took a lot away from the teamwork: it can be challenging, but it is also extremely enriching. My 
biggest takeaway would be to stay curious, push yourself out of your comfort zone and invest fully in the experience.”

## IMPACT Excellence participant

“For me, IMPACT Accelerate was about learning, excellence, hard work and resilience. One of the biggest growth areas 
was understanding how to manage a team, assessing the strengths and weaknesses of different people and finding ways 
to work together to get the best out of everyone. One message that really stayed with me is that we can disagree, but we 
can also ‘disagree agreeably’ and with respect. This is important not only at work, but in all aspects of life.”

## IMPACT Accelerate participant

“For me, IMPACT Catalyst was a human adventure: learning from others, connecting with colleagues and sharing our 
knowledge. The programme encouraged me to step out of my comfort zone, and I learned how to be adaptive while 
staying focused on my goals. I also developed stronger problem solving skills, including learning how to understand the 
challenges and perspectives of different stakeholders and deal with people with empathy. This journey shaped me.”

## Shaping a Strong, Adaptive and Inclusive Culture

We believe that fostering the right culture is critical to helping us win in the workplace. This includes creating an 
environment where people bring their best to achieve shared goals.

## Diversity, equity and inclusion

Building on our progress to create a more gender-equitable workplace, we are broadening our diversity, equity and inclusion 
(DEI) approach to help create an environment where every employee feels valued, respected and able to contribute.

This year, we strengthened the governance structures and accountability needed to embed DEI more systematically across 
the organisation. Building on the foundations established in FY25, we focused on increasing employee participation and 
integrating DEI considerations into people processes, policy frameworks and business decision-making.

## A key milestone was formalising our DEI Committee:

Established last year, we formalised the DEI Committee’s governance arrangements this year, supported by approved terms 
of reference and a governance framework aligned with the Group’s strategic priorities, human rights commitments and 
relevant labour standards. The committee meets quarterly and is responsible for setting DEI priorities, validating initiatives 
proposed by ERGs, monitoring progress against agreed objectives and reviewing emerging DEI risks and opportunities.

## We launched the DEI Charter:

Building on the Gender Equality Charter established in 2021, our DEI Charter formalises the Group’s commitments to equity, 
inclusive leadership, fair rewards, well-being, non-discrimination and accountability. It provides a common framework for 
employees, leaders and stakeholders while supporting MCB’s participation in broader national and regional DEI initiatives.

Our Employee Resource Groups (ERGs) continued to help shape our DEI priorities and initiatives:

Our ERGs provide voluntary, employee-led platforms for colleagues to connect, share perspectives and contribute to a more 
inclusive and responsive workplace. During the year, employees from across the Group choose to join and help shape the 
activities of our three ERGs: Disability Friendly, Gender Equity and Belonging. Their input complements our wider listening 
mechanisms and helps bring employee perspectives into discussions on culture, inclusion and well-being.

## Our inclusion efforts extended beyond the workplace:

This year, we launched MCB Bridges under our ERG Disability Friendly and began examining accessibility across our 
products, services and physical infrastructure, broadening our focus on inclusion to the experiences of our customers and 
communities.

## Gender equality

We remain committed to creating a more gender-equitable workplace and increasing the representation of women 
in leadership roles.

We set a target to achieve 40% women at middle and senior management levels by December 2026 (MCB Ltd). Aligned 
with this, we aimed to achieve 35% women in managerial roles by December 2024 to meet the targets of our inaugural 
Sustainability Linked Syndicated Term Loan signed in December 2023. As at June 2026, 35.1% of our middle and senior 
management level roles were held by women.

## Our People

While representation of women in leadership has increased over time, we do not expect to reach our 40% target by 
December 2026.

Progress has been slower than anticipated, reflecting challenges in attracting women into some senior roles and in 
converting the internal female talent pipeline into middle and senior management positions. These challenges also 
highlighted the limitations of applying a single target across different management levels and career paths.

During FY26, we strengthened our focus on the underlying drivers of representation through more regular monitoring of 
recruitment, promotion and succession data, stronger succession and talent pipeline planning and the continued application 
of inclusive recruitment practices. We are also reviewing our approach to introduce more differentiated, data-led targets and 
clearer milestones, supported by stronger accountability.

Our focus also broadened beyond representation alone, with discussions increasingly considering wider measures of 
workplace fairness and career equity. Our efforts were supported by our Gender Equality ERG, which conducted a range 
of awareness and engagement initiatives during the year, including Women’s Month, Movember and Pride Month. Looking 
ahead, an Inclusive Mentoring framework is being developed for rollout in FY27.

MCB maintained its EQUAL-SALARY certification this year, confirming 
continued compliance with international pay equity standards.

We implement fair and equitable pay based on performance, with equal pay for 
equal work, irrespective of gender. The certification process includes a salary 
analysis to assess unexplained differences in pay between women and men.  
To meet the certification requirements, the unexplained wage difference must be 
5% or less. MCB meets this requirement.

First awarded in May 2024, MCB was the first African bank to obtain EQUAL-SALARY 
certification.

## Performance management

We embed career development within our performance management process to support a high-performance 
culture, underpinned by targeted learning and specialised training opportunities.

Employees across the Group engage in a guided annual success and review process, ensuring alignment between individual 
objectives and business priorities. Our Values and Shared Ways of Working are fully embedded within the performance 
management process, reinforcing alignment between what employees deliver and how they achieve results. Through career 
conversations, our People Managers guide and coach employees, leveraging resources that enable them to reflect on their 
goals, identify growth opportunities and build actionable development plans for long-term success.

## Employee well-being

At MCB, success is about more than performance. It is also about creating an environment where our people feel 
supported, connected and able to thrive. We therefore take a holistic approach to employee well-being across four 
interconnected dimensions: physical, mental, financial and social well-being. 
 
Our Wellness Unit coordinates initiatives across these four dimensions and provides employees with guidance and support 
on well-being-related matters. Through practical resources, shared experiences and opportunities to connect, we aim to 
embed well-being in the broader employee experience we seek to create through #WorkingAtMCB.

## Key initiatives undertaken in FY26

•	
Healthy ‘YOU’: Expert-led health talks, fitness activities and wellness opportunities. 500+ employees attended health 
talks, 100+ employees participated in fitness activities, and 700+ employees took part in health screenings.

•	
FamiLink: Celebrates and recognises the importance of family in our employees’ lives. 3000 individuals attended the 
celebration (1500 parents and 1500 children).

•	
Movember: Throughout November, we delivered a series of initiatives focused on men’s health and well-being, including 
expert-led talks and awareness activities. The campaign encouraged open conversations and provided practical 
information and support on key health topics, recording 340 attendances across the various initiatives.

•	
Sports@MCB Awards Ceremony: An evening dedicated to recognising staff who represent our brand across Federation 
de Maurice des Sports Corporatifs sports competitions. Celebrating over 20 athletes from various disciplines, the evening 
was a proud moment of unity, honouring their dedication and the pride they bring to MCB.

•	
IWD26: International Women’s Day was marked throughout March through a series of initiatives supporting women’s 
well-being, development and financial empowerment. Activities included a golf initiation session and a financial well-
being workshop, with 949 attendances recorded across the month.

## Strengthening well-being, connection and belonging

Throughout the year, we created opportunities for our people to connect beyond their day-to-day roles, reinforcing a 
culture where connection, collaboration and inclusion are valued.

## Copa Cola Cup 2026

Around 1,600 employees came together for Copa Cola Cup 2026, taking part in sporting, creative and team-based activities. 
With participation spanning business units, entities and geographies, the event encouraged collaboration and created 
memorable shared experiences that reinforced team spirit, pride and belonging.

## Celebrating long-standing service

We recognised colleagues reaching significant career milestones, including 25 and 40 years of service, and celebrated 
employees entering retirement. These moments provided an opportunity to honour their commitment, contribution and the 
lasting legacy they have created at MCB.

Fair Compensation and Benefits  
 
Our remuneration philosophy

The Group aims to promote fair and competitive remuneration that encourages performance and assists in 
attracting and retaining talent. Our remuneration policy is based on meritocracy and ensures that:

•	
Comprehensive protection is provided at the lower end of the income ladder against cost-of-living increases.

•	
Fairness and equity are promoted throughout the organisation.

•	
Opportunity is given to employees to benefit from the Group’s financial results and development:

•	
Staff members of the Group receive an annual discretionary bonus based on the Group’s performance and their 
own individual contribution. Staff members who benefit from the annual discretionary bonus have the added 
opportunity to benefit from an employee share option scheme.

## Our remuneration package

The remuneration package is anchored on a range of factors including qualifications, skills scarcity, past performance, 
individual potential, market practices, responsibilities shouldered and experience. With a view to defining appropriate 
remuneration levels, the Group is also guided by the following considerations:

•	
Market conditions are regularly surveyed to ensure that remuneration packages are motivating and competitive.

•	
Superior team performance is highly encouraged and rewarded with adequate incentives.

## Mechanisms for Managing Employee Complaints

We recognise that open communication and fair resolution of concerns are essential to employee well-being.  
 
Our Grievance Policy is an important part of our commitment to creating and maintaining a safe, supportive and respectful 
workplace. This policy and its associated procedures ensure that our employees are aware of internal mechanisms designed 
to handle grievances efficiently. The framework equips complainants with a clear and accessible process to express any 
concerns with their respective Line Managers or any other designated person.

1
2
3
4

If still unresolved, a 
committee is established 
comprising the relevant 
SBU Manager and HR 
representatives. All 
parties must ensure 
they reach an outcome 
that is reasonable, fair 
and equitable to both 
the employee and the 
business.

Employees report 
grievances to their 
Line Managers. 
Once received, 
the complaint is 
acknowledged and an 
investigation initiated.

Once all relevant 
evidence is obtained, 
Line Managers 
assess whether 
further investigation 
is required. Where 
appropriate, the 
necessary disciplinary 
action is implemented.

If unresolved, 
grievances are 
escalated to BU 
Managers.

For steps three and four, employees may request the presence of their Union representative. At every stage of our grievance 
process, we take measures to ensure that relevant parties are separated pending completion of the inquiry.

To ensure remedial actions are implemented and tracked, our Employee Relations Team provides recommendations to the 
appropriate Line Managers or BU Managers and guides managers on the implementation of remedial actions, including the 
identification of emerging risks. Once grievances are addressed, our Employee Relations Team conducts regular follow-ups 
to monitor remedial actions and ensure affected employees are safely back at work.

During FY26, 41 investigations were conducted, with six investigations ongoing as at 31 July 2026.

We continually review our Grievance Policy to ensure that it remains relevant and aligned with applicable legislative 
requirements, and no amendments were made to the policy during FY26.

Our Grievance Policy applies to MCB Group. Grievance mechanisms are managed internally by our local and 
overseas banking subsidiaries, with the Group sharing best practices and providing harmonisation, where 
required.
