## Our People

## Occupational Health and Safety

At MCB, we believe that safety is a shared responsibility, and we remain committed to providing our employees with 
a healthy and safe working environment.

Our approach to health and safety aligns with Mauritius’ Occupational Safety and Health Act 2005 (OSHA 2005) and its 
accompanying regulations. We outline our approach in our Occupational Safety and Health Policy Statement, which applies 
to MCB Group and local subsidiaries.

## The key elements of our policy statement focus on:

•	
Compliance: We adhere to OSHA 2005, including establishing health and safety committees throughout our operations, 
which report to relevant management and oversight committees.

•	
Commitment: We will allocate the necessary resources to support our company’s safety initiatives.

•	
Practice and management: We integrate health and safety into our business practices and processes as well as our 
management systems. This includes proactively managing health and safety risks and implementing preventive actions 
to keep our employees healthy and safe.

•	
Education: We provide training to employees to ensure they are aware of health and safety matters and understand 
potential risks, and we equip them with knowledge on how to manage these risks.

•	
Access: We ensure our employees can access our reporting systems and notify us of any potential hazards.

During the year, MCB Ltd recorded 35 (FY25: 26) reported injuries mainly caused by accidental falls. These injuries  
resulted in 26 lost workdays, 20 were attributable to male employees and 6 to female employees.

## Enhanced healthcare services

We collaborate with Médecin à Domicile, a healthcare organisation that provides medical services to our employees and their 
eligible dependents. These services include on-site doctor consultations, remote medical assistance, advice by telephone and 
home visits, where required.

We expanded this partnership this year by increasing the number of on-site medical consultation sessions across MCB 
premises from 12 to 16 per month, improving access to medical advice and early intervention. We also introduced a cashless 
home-based inpatient care service for selected medical conditions, enabling eligible beneficiaries to receive appropriate 
treatment at home as an alternative to hospital admission. This supports a more patient-centred approach to care and more 
efficient healthcare utilisation.

In FY26, we registered 644 on-site consultations for physical healthcare services (FY25: 686) as well as 593 appointments at 
our on-site psychological service (FY25: 371).

## Supporting early intervention and healthier choice

## Preventive health screening through BIOSANTÉ

This year, we launched a health screening initiative as part of our Healthy ‘YOU’ campaign in partnership with 
BIOSANTÉ. Registered employees and their dependants received access to cashless preferential blood screening 
services, encouraging greater health awareness while supporting early detection of potential health risks.

## MCB Rodrigues Health Campaign

We conducted a two-day health campaign in Rodrigues. Participants received consultations with a general 
practitioner and a paediatrician, complemented by a health awareness session. The campaign aimed to 
strengthen employees understanding of health issues and encourage healthier lifestyle choices.

1 Environmental DNA monitoring is a non-invasive technique where scientists collect samples from nature, such as water, soil or air, and extract genetic material shed by 
organisms (via skin, waste or mucus) to identify species and assess biodiversity.

2 A geographically defined space outside a formal protected area that achieves positive, long-term outcomes for biodiversity conservation.  
These sites deliver effective conservation as a primary, secondary or ancillary result of their management, helping nations meet global biodiversity goals.

3 Mauritius’ Exclusive Economic Zone is a maritime area, covering roughly 1.9 to 2.3 million square kilometres of the Indian Ocean. Mauritius 
holds sovereign rights to explore, manage and protect both living and non-living resources within this zone.

## Odysseo Marine Foundation

This year, MCB announced a new partnership with the Odysseo Marine Foundation, extending our support for 
initiatives that protect Mauritius’ marine ecosystems while promoting community engagement, innovation and 
entrepreneurship.

Focus
Established
Sponsorship

## Marine conservation and

restoration
FY26
FY26: Rs 2 million

Through our partnership with the Odysseo Marine Foundation, we contribute to the Ocean Restoration Economic Centre. 
This newly-established space is designed to bring together a wide community of stakeholders to support the development of 
Mauritius’ blue economy within the country’s Exclusive Economic Zone3.

This includes exploring innovative blue finance initiatives, such as establishing an incubator for blue economy start-ups. 
The centre also supports scientific research and collaboration, including through the University of Mauritius/Odysseo 
Marine Station launched in January 2026, which provides a dedicated facility for researchers working on the sustainable and 
regenerative use of ocean resources.

MCB aims to leverage its capabilities in fostering entrepreneurship and developing innovative products and services to 
promote the sustainable use of ocean resources for the benefit of communities and future generations.

## Charles Telfair Centre

We are a Platinum Sponsor of the Charles Telfair Centre, a multidisciplinary platform for knowledge sharing and 
debate on topics relevant to Mauritius and the region’s development.

Rs 800,000 
(FY25: Rs 800,000)

## Sponsorship activities

A highlight of our sponsorship in FY26 was organising a conference and panel discussion on ‘Financing a Sustainable Future: 
Unlocking Capital for Resilience and Growth in the Western Indian Ocean’. The event brought together around 
100 participants, including regulators, financial institutions, sustainability specialists and business leaders.

Discussions explored innovative financing solutions alongside the enabling conditions required to mobilise capital for 
sustainable development. The event further reinforced the importance of public-private collaboration in unlocking the 
region’s blue and green economy potential and supporting longer-term resilience.

Additionally, our funding supported operational activities, including communication, as well as six workshops addressing 
topics such as labour migration, circular waste management and competitive and sustainable ports.

“We are delighted to have MCB supporting the Charles Telfair Centre as a platinum sponsor. Their backing 
has allowed us to bring stakeholders together across the region around some of the key questions that 
matter for the Western Indian Ocean region, such as how to build a more sustainable and regenerative 
tourism sector, how to pursue peaceful ocean diplomacy or how to move towards zero ocean plastic. Their 
support also helps us think through what it takes to become a research-driven nation, one that serves the 
private sector, government and civil society alike so that decisions rest on sound science. Most importantly, 
their support lets us keep knowledge free and open to everyone, which feeds the virtuous circle of 
knowledge creation.”

## Myriam Blin, Head of the Charles Telfair Centre

## Deba Klima

Hosted in collaboration with the Rajiv Gandhi Science Centre and Dr François Gemenne, Deba Klima is a debate 
competition for secondary school students that aims to build young people’s knowledge of climate change and 
encourage them to explore collaborative solutions.

The debates are conducted in Mauritian Creole, placing the language at the heart of the conversations and making complex 
climate issues more accessible and relatable to the everyday lives of Mauritians and our communities.

Beyond developing debating and communication skills, participants deepen their understanding of climate science, policy, 
economics and the social dimensions of climate change. In doing so, the programme seeks to equip young people to engage 
more confidently with climate issues and become informed advocates for action.

## Increasing community and youth engagement

This year, we introduced ‘Zenes Kapav’, a campaign that aimed to deepen youth participation in Deba Klima and 
encourage continued engagement with climate issues beyond the competition.

Two former finalists were selected as campaign ambassadors. During the year, they visited schools to engage 
with students, share debating tips and gather participant reflections following debates. The campaign included a 
Facebook broadcast channel, providing behind-the-scenes content and interviews with ambassadors, teachers and 
participating students.

## Employee community support

Our contribution to stronger, more resilient communities extends beyond financial support.

We create opportunities for our employees to make an impact through our social leave programme and targeted community 
initiatives, enabling them to contribute their time, skills and resources in response to local needs while reinforcing a culture 
of shared responsibility.

## Employee social leave programme

In 2019, we launched our employee social leave programme, which gives our employees one paid leave day each 
year to volunteer and work alongside our partners to uplift communities.

Activities are designed around community partner needs and range from conservation and clean-up initiatives to 
maintenance projects and engagement with community organisations. Beyond volunteering, our social leave programme 
encourages team bonding and collaboration, driving personal and collective impact.

## Key activities undertaken during the year

We continued to offer a range of volunteering opportunities throughout the year, including larger quarterly activities.  
We focused on embedding a more personalised volunteering approach to encourage participation and help colleagues 
develop a stronger volunteering spirit. We further leveraged our internal communication platform to promote social impact 
initiatives, helping to raise awareness and foster employee engagement in activities that support our communities.

## MCB Madagascar

Launched its first social leave activities in FY26, creating new 
opportunities for employees to volunteer in local communities. 
Employees supported EPP Djabala Be primary school in Nosy Be 
and the ASAMA educational programme in Antananarivo through 
donations, classroom support and student recreational activities. 
Building on these initiatives, the Bank plans to organise two social 
leave activities each year across Antananarivo and other regions to 
broaden geographical reach and widen employee participation.

MCB Madagascar also entered into an annual partnership 
with the Université Catholique de Madagascar during the year, 
supporting its ongoing commitment to local communities and the 
development of Malagasy youth.

Following Tropical Cyclone Gezani in February 2026, MCB 
Madagascar coordinated support for 400 vulnerable households 
through a partnership with the Development Office of the 
Diocese of Toamasina, Caritas Internationalis and local partners. 
Assistance included essential supplies, mobile health clinics, 
support to restore livelihoods and the construction of  
20 wooden houses along the Pangalanes Canal.

Together with MCB Group, MCB Madagascar continued to co-fund 
ADRA’s7 WASH Project, supporting improved access to clean water 
and sanitation across nine rural communities in Farafangana. In 
FY26, construction began on nine water points and five sanitation 
blocks, 80 local water committee members were trained, and 
more than 16,000 beneficiaries were reached through hygiene-
awareness activities. MCB Madagascar also partnered with KM du 
Cœur to upgrade WASH infrastructure at two primary schools in 
Manjakandriana, benefiting approximately 170 students and six 
teachers.

in community partnerships this year (FY25: MGA 35 million;

## MCB Forward Foundation

The MCB Forward Foundation (MCBFF) is our corporate social responsibility arm, supporting social inclusion and 
well-being through meaningful partnerships, with a focus on empowering people and reducing social inequalities.

MCBFF fosters sustainable communities by collaborating with committed and diverse stakeholders, including educators, 
health professionals, NGOs and government bodies. MCBFF also supports its implementation partners by providing advisory 
services, logistical support and staff training to strengthen community projects and ensure long-term impact. This is 
reinforced by ongoing engagement, helping partners enhance project delivery and monitor progress.

During FY26, MCBFF continued to fund projects that align with the Group’s purpose and values. The amount entrusted to 
MCBFF as at June 2026 was Rs 94.1 million from MCB Group, of which Rs 90.1 million9 was from MCB Limited and Rs 4 million 
was from other local subsidiaries. MCBFF invested Rs 76.5 million in 42 projects this year.

We provide an overview of key projects on the following pages of this report, with a full project summary available in Appendix 5: 
Community Investment Summary.

## MCB Football Academy

We recognise the critical role that education plays in individual and community well-being, prosperity and resilience. 
Through the MCB Football Academy, we support child development through sports and education. We also empower 
families by helping them embrace their role as caregivers and active members of society.

Established in 2008 in the village of St-Hilaire, MCBFF launched the MCB Football Academy as a social initiative that uses 
football as a universal language to promote social integration through sports, mentorship and education. Today, the 
academy has expanded to include four academies across Mauritius and three in Rodrigues. Children who demonstrate 
strong sporting potential may be selected to join the MCB Elite Team, offering them the opportunity to further develop 
their skills and join other regional clubs and the national football teams.

## MCB Agro-Ecological Farm

The MCB Agro-Ecological Farm aims to serve as a model for environmentally responsible and economically viable 
agriculture that strengthens food security and benefits people and the planet.

Region
FY26 sponsorship
FY26 beneficiaries

Mauritius (Riche-en-Eau)
Rs 2.9 million   
(Funded by MCBFF)

11 
direct beneficiaries

Operational since 2021, the farm serves as a training centre for its workers and partner organisations, building skills in agro-
ecological, organic farming practices and creating job opportunities for the community and parents of the MCB Football 
Academy. Training and technical support are provided by Terres d’Agroécologie and the Food and Agricultural Research and 
Extension Institute (FAREI).

The farm was awarded Terres d’Agroécologie’s Agroecological Quality Label in April 2025, with this recognition renewed 
in August 2026 for a second consecutive year. The farm was the first in Mauritius to be certified against the specification, 
affirming its high labour and production standards and validating our ability to build a replicable agricultural model that 
serves people and communities.

In partnership with FAREI, the farm continues to explore opportunities to cultivate climate-resilient local seed varieties that 
support agricultural biodiversity conservation and restoration, thereby promoting long-term food security.

## Sponsorship activities

During FY26, the farm established a collaboration with CIRAD, a French agricultural research and cooperation organisation 
focused on the sustainable development of tropical and Mediterranean regions. Through this partnership, MCB Agro-
Ecological Farm is contributing technical input and field-based insights to the DESIRA+OI initiative9, which aims to accelerate 
the agro-ecological transition of food systems across the Indian Ocean region.

## Arts and Culture

At MCB, we continue to invest in protecting and promoting Mauritius’ rich cultural heritage, while supporting 
local talent and initiatives that celebrate the diversity and creativity of our communities.

## Blue Penny Museum

Founded by MCB in 2001, the Blue Penny Museum celebrates Mauritius’ history and cultural heritage through a collection 
of artworks, artefacts, books, coins and stamps built over more than 185 years. Beyond preserving this heritage, the Museum 
brings it to life for visitors from Mauritius and abroad as well as younger generations. This includes offering immersive 
learning experiences that complement the national curriculum across primary and secondary levels.

Named after the iconic Blue 
Pence and Red Penny stamps 
of 1847, the Museum is the only 
place in the world where visitors 
can view the two original, non-
obliterated10 stamps side by side.

In FY26, the Blue Penny Museum welcomed  
28,600 visitors.

This year, the Blue Penny Museum introduced a refreshed team structure, reflecting the Museum’s commitment 
to ongoing transformation. The strengthened team brings additional expertise to key areas including audience 
engagement and collections management, while encouraging greater cross-generational collaboration, innovation 
and continuous improvement.

## Archive digitisation project

In 2023, we launched a major digitisation project to establish a more detailed inventory of items in the museum’s collection, 
reflecting our commitment to safeguarding and sharing Mauritius’ cultural heritage and making it more accessible to the 
public through dedicated exhibitions.

The Museum’s digitised collection is available on an internal digital collection management system, facilitating easier access 
to collection information and imagery and enabling the Museum to respond more efficiently to research requests or share 
material for books, films and other projects. The next stage of the project will focus on exploring how best to make the 
digitised collection more widely accessible to the public.

## Our Communities

“The digitalisation project has made my role as Museum Collections Coordinator much easier. Having access 
to a digital database allows me to understand, analyse and share our collections more efficiently with both 
colleagues and external partners. It is exciting to see the museum move beyond traditional analogue methods 
and embrace new technologies that help us better preserve, manage and showcase our heritage.”

## Ilina Rughoobur – Museum Collections Coordinator

This year, the Museum participated in an Expertise France initiative focused on the preservation and promotion of 
Mauritius’ architectural heritage. Through collaborative workshops, the Museum contributed expertise in heritage 
conservation, collection management and public engagement to support the development of a more coordinated 
and sustainable approach to heritage preservation. This participation reinforces the Museum’s role as a key 
stakeholder in the national heritage ecosystem.

## Initiative spotlight: Artizan Lamer

This year, the Museum hosted Artizan Lamer, a temporary exhibition celebrating the craftsmanship, knowledge  
and traditions of Mauritian fishing communities.

Paired with workshops and community activities, the exhibition raised awareness of Mauritius’ maritime heritage and the 
cultural importance of artisanal fishing communities, while creating opportunities to share traditional skills and knowledge. 
Activities included casier and pirogue-making and workshops on fishing techniques. The Museum also hosted sessions in 
partnership with local organisations, bringing community members together to share local traditions and stories.

The Museum has continued its relationship with participating artisans beyond the exhibition, with their products now 
available in the Museum boutique. Building on the initiative, it plans to develop further community-focused exhibitions 
showcasing local skills and traditions.

Responsible and 
Ethical Business 
Practices

We recognise that responsible behaviour is central 
to safeguarding our reputation, earning stakeholder 
trust and sustaining our long-term success. Our 
commitment to business ethics and integrity 
underpins how we work, make decisions and deliver 
on our purpose.

We embed policies, controls and procedures across the Group to support 
compliance with applicable laws, rules and regulations. We reinforce a culture of 
professionalism, integrity and accountability through ongoing employee training 
and awareness, supported by mechanisms for raising concerns and addressing 
misconduct.

Our responsibility extends to how we engage with customers, communities and 
business partners. We seek to deliver fair and responsible customer outcomes, 
protect customer information and strengthen awareness of fraud and financial 
crime. Across our supply chain, we promote responsible sourcing and engage with 
our suppliers to uphold appropriate standards of ethical conduct and responsible 
business practice.

Together, these measures support a broader ecosystem of trust, transparency and 
responsible behaviour.

## Policy Overview

We are committed to conducting business ethically, responsibly and in compliance 
with all applicable legislation, regulations, adopted industry codes and standards 
and adhering to all internal policies and sound corporate governance principles.

## Policy review and approval process

To uphold the highest standards of business ethics and regulatory compliance, we ensure that all policies are regularly 
reviewed, approved and effectively communicated to the relevant internal and external stakeholders. Our policies undergo a 
comprehensive review process to ensure alignment with industry best practices, applicable legal and regulatory requirements 
and our strategic objectives, risk appetite and reputation.

The review process occurs at various levels depending on the policy’s scope and impact:

## Internal and external communication

We believe in transparent communication and take required steps to ensure all employees and relevant external 
stakeholders are informed about our policies.

For our employees and internal stakeholders, we use various channels to communicate our policies, such as the 
company’s intranet, internal communication platforms, awareness campaigns and training sessions. Policies relevant to 
external stakeholders are shared through public channels such as our corporate website. Where appropriate, we also 
engage directly with affected parties via dedicated communication channels.

Consistent with this approach, the retirement of policies is communicated internally through the same dedicated channels to 
ensure clarity and alignment.

## Business Ethics and Integrity

At MCB Group, we recognise that upholding the trust and confidence of our employees, customers, shareholders and 
investors, as well as the economies, societies and communities we serve, is a fundamental pillar to our continued success. 
We promote a culture of shared responsibility for business ethics and integrity, fostering a workplace built on trust, 
transparency and continuous improvement.

Sets the tone from the top by consistently modelling purpose-driven and ethical behaviour, mutual 
respect and professional excellence, thereby reinforcing a culture where integrity is non-negotiable 
and accountability is shared. All new directors joining the Group participate in a comprehensive 
induction programme, with a strong foundation in business ethics and integrity.

## Looking Ahead

In FY27, we will roll out a Group-wide Ethics Training programme to strengthen ethical business 
practices and reinforce adherence to our Code of Ethics and Business Conduct. The initiative will 
include a mandatory e-learning module covering ethical behaviour, key drivers and consequences 
of misconduct, common ethics and compliance failures, employee responsibilities, confidentiality 
obligations and mechanisms for reporting ethical concerns.

## Consequences of violating the Code

Non-compliance with the Code may have severe consequences for MCB Group and all employees, officers and directors. 
Where the Group deems it appropriate, disciplinary or preventive actions may be taken to address existing or potential Code 
violations.

## Reporting channels

Open communication of issues and concerns by all without fear of retribution is crucial. Internal and external stakeholders 
may report concerns to MCB Group’s respective subsidiaries via dedicated channels. Concerns may be reported 
confidentially and anonymously via our Speak Up channel or through our Whistleblowing platform. MCB Group forbids 
victimisation and/or retaliation for reports or complaints regarding misconduct when made in good faith.

## Bribery and Corruption

The Group has a zero-tolerance approach towards 
bribery and corruption and promotes the highest 
standards of integrity and ethical conduct across its 
operations.  
 
At MCB Ltd, this commitment is formalised through 
the Bank’s Anti-Bribery and Corruption Policy, which 
strictly prohibits employees from soliciting, accepting or 
receiving any form of gratification in the course of business 
that could compromise, or appear to compromise, their 
duties towards clients, shareholders, business partners, 
employees and other stakeholders. The Bank prohibits any 
payments, gifts, or benefits to public officials intended to 
influence official decisions or secure approvals.

The policy further sets out clear expectations for integrity-
based business practices and provides practical guidance 
to help employees assess the appropriateness of specific 
situations and maintain compliance with the Bank’s ethical 
standards.

Across the wider Group, the underlying principles of 
the policy continue to be promoted, with Group entities 
encouraged to align with these standards and implement 
appropriate anti-bribery and corruption frameworks in 
accordance with local regulatory requirements.

To reinforce our culture of integrity and transparency, members of the leadership team, designated employees, BU 
Managers and SBU Heads are required annually to submit gift declarations and attest their compliance with the Anti-
Bribery and Corruption Policy. During the year, we further strengthened our governance framework through the launch 
of a dedicated Gift Declaration Platform via MCB’s intranet, providing a streamlined and transparent mechanism for the 
reporting, review and monitoring of gifts.

No material incidents of bribery or corruption were recorded in FY26.

Anti-bribery and corruption awareness is embedded within the Group’s mandatory induction programme for all new 
employees, reinforcing the importance of ethical conduct and integrity from the outset of employment.

Looking Ahead 
In FY27, we will further strengthen our anti-bribery and corruption framework through the rollout 
of a dedicated training programme for all employees, aimed at enhancing awareness, reinforcing 
individual accountability and embedding effective implementation of related controls and standards.

## Responsible and ethical business practices

## Fraud

As we navigate an increasingly complex and evolving 
digital landscape, effective fraud prevention 
remains critical to protecting the Group’s assets and 
safeguarding the trust and financial well-being of our 
customers.  
 
Complying with relevant rules and standards in personal 
and business conduct is the responsibility of every 
employee. Our Fraud Policy outlines our approach 
to managing compliance risk, defines employee and 
management responsibilities and sets out the controls to 
support fraud detection and prevention. Employees are 
supported through training and tools to help them identify, 
report and prevent all forms of fraud.

We review and update our Fraud Policy to ensure it 
remains appropriate, responsive to prevailing legislation 
and aligned with organisational best practice. Last year, 
the annual review of the Policy was postponed due to 
a leadership change. During FY26, the review remained 
pending while the Bank progressed work to establish an 
enhanced governance and operational framework for fraud 
management across MCB. Once the relevant framework, 
roles and responsibilities have been established, the Policy 
will be reviewed and updated to ensure alignment with the 
Bank’s fraud risk management objectives and regulatory 
requirements.

fraud 
cases

strengthened customer verification and authentication, particularly during

theft, impersonation and account takeover fraud. Phishing remained the most

## Training, monitoring and reinforcement

Employee awareness  
 
Employee awareness was reinforced through targeted campaigns aligned with recognised awareness periods, using 
messages, articles and videos shared across the Bank’s internal channels to strengthen understanding of internal audit and 
fraud-related risks.

•	 IIA Awareness Month: as part of the month’s broader focus on raising awareness of the internal audit profession, MCB 
strengthened employees’ understanding of Internal Audit’s role in governance, risk management and internal controls, 
while reinforcing collaboration between Internal Audit and business functions.

•	 International Fraud Awareness Week: aligned with the global initiative to raise awareness of fraud prevention, MCB 
focused on emerging fraud risks, common scam techniques and cyber-enabled threats, while reinforcing employees’ role 
in fraud prevention and detection.

## Customer and community awareness

Recognising that consumers increasingly face complex financial decisions and heightened exposure to digital and financial 
fraud risks, MCB continued to support financial literacy and fraud awareness through targeted campaigns, educational 
sessions and fraud awareness initiatives for customers, students and NGOs. These initiatives aimed to equip participants 
with the knowledge and skills needed to make informed financial decisions, protect themselves against scams and fraud, and 
navigate the evolving financial landscape responsibly.

MCB’s financial literacy initiatives are closely aligned with the Bank of Mauritius’s Financial Literacy Strategy and its broader 
objective of promoting a stable, sound and resilient financial system.

Read more about our approach to building financial literacy in communities on page 100.

## Responsible and ethical business practices

## Customer Privacy and Information Security

Customer privacy is a material topic for MCB Group due to the highly sensitive nature of the personal and financial 
data that we handle in our role as a banking and financial services institution.

MCB Group places utmost priority on the confidentiality, integrity and availability of information. We are committed to 
protecting the privacy and security of personal and financial data belonging to our customers, employees and stakeholders. 
As a leader in the banking and financial services industry, we acknowledge that data privacy is fundamental to maintaining 
trust and complying with evolving legal and regulatory standards.

## Data Privacy Policy and Framework

We are committed to maintaining a consistent data privacy framework across the organisation.

Our Data Privacy Policy was reviewed during the Group’s annual policy review to ensure it reflects current regulatory 
requirements and aligns with best practice. It was approved by the Board in May 2026. This overarching policy is accessible 
internally to all employees. Our approach to governing data protection and processing is set out in our Privacy Notice, 
available on our website. The Privacy Notice was reviewed and updated in December 2025.

Our Data Privacy Policy is aligned with the Mauritius Data Protection Act (DPA) 2017 and the European Union General Data 
Protection Regulation (GDPR). It applies to all MCB employees and contractors who handle personal data in any form (paper 
or electronic). The policy covers data related to past, present and prospective employees, customers, suppliers, business 
partners, contractors, sub-contractors and any third parties.

Access the Privacy Notice here.

## Data subject rights

MCB is committed to upholding the rights of data subjects, including customers, employees, suppliers, contractors and 
business partners, under the DPA and, where applicable, the GDPR. These rights include:

•	
Right to be Informed: Individuals are notified through clear and concise privacy notices of the purpose, legal basis, use, 
processing, sharing and retention of their personal data.

•	
Right of Access: Individuals may request access to their personal data and to information about how it is processed, 
including access to information held in recordings of calls and meetings. Requests are addressed within one month 
(extendable by a further month where the request is complex) and are handled under MCB’s established Right of Access 
procedure. The Data Protection Officer (DPO) may decline requests that are manifestly excessive, applying the criteria 
defined in the Data Privacy Policy.

•	
Right to Rectification: Inaccurate or incomplete personal data is corrected without undue delay. This obligation is 
embedded in MCB’s routine business processes.

189
MCB Group Limited | Sustainability Report 2026


---
*Page 191*


•	
Right to Erasure (Right to be Forgotten): Individuals may request deletion of their personal data where it is no longer 
necessary for the purpose for which it was collected; where consent is withdrawn and no other legal basis applies; 
where the data has been processed unlawfully; where the individual objects to processing for direct marketing; or where 
erasure is required to comply with a legal obligation. Erasure does not apply where MCB is required to retain the data 
under applicable banking, AML/CFT or other legal and regulatory obligations.

•	
Right to Restrict Processing: Individuals may request that processing be restricted where: the accuracy of the data 
is contested, pending verification by MCB; the processing is unlawful and the individual prefers restriction to erasure; 
MCB no longer needs the data for its original purpose, but the individual requires it to be retained to establish, exercise 
or defend a legal claim; or the individual has objected to the processing, pending MCB’s assessment of whether it has 
overriding legitimate grounds.

•	
Right to Object: Individuals may object to the processing of their personal data carried out on the basis of legitimate 
interests, for direct marketing purposes, or for scientific, historical or statistical purposes. Objections to direct marketing 
are always upheld and processing ceases without exception. For other objections, MCB will cease processing unless 
it can demonstrate compelling legitimate grounds that override the individual’s interests, or where the processing is 
necessary for the establishment, exercise or defence of a legal claim or for a task carried out in the public interest.

•	
Right to Data Portability: Where processing is based on consent or on the performance of a contract and is carried out 
by automated means, individuals covered by the GDPR may request that their personal data be provided, or transmitted 
to another controller, in a structured, commonly used and machine-readable format.

•	
Rights Related to Automated Decision-Making: Where a decision producing legal effects or similarly significant 
impacts is taken solely by automated means, including profiling, MCB informs the individual, provides a mechanism to 
obtain human intervention, and allows the individual to express his or her point of view and to contest the decision.

Requests may be submitted to the Group DPO, who oversees an internal procedure ensuring that all these rights are 
supported and upheld. Individuals also retain the right to lodge a complaint with the Data Protection Commissioner.

## Governance and oversight

The Cyber and Technology Risk Committee (CTRC) assists the Board in overseeing compliance with all applicable laws 
and regulations relevant to cyber, information and technology risks, including, but not limited to, the DPA (2017) and the 
Guideline on Cyber and Technology Risk Management issued by the Bank of Mauritius. The CTRC is a joint committee 
comprising the Board of Directors of MCB Ltd and MCB Group Ltd. The committee’s responsibilities and governance 
framework are outlined in the CTRC Charter available on the website.

Access the CTRC Charter here.

## Data breach management

MCB has a formal Data Breach Management Process to identify, respond to and monitor breaches promptly within the 
timeframe prescribed. This includes escalation protocols, notification procedures to supervisory authorities and affected data 
subjects. The process includes proactive and reactive measures and follows the personal data breach guidelines specified in 
the DPA (2017).

During FY26, 18 personal data breaches were reported to the Group DPO. All were assessed as minor in 
nature, were promptly contained and resolved, and had minimal to no adverse impact on the rights and 
freedoms of the data subjects concerned.

## Three lines of defence for data privacy and cybersecurity

Our cybersecurity and data privacy framework is supported by a robust governance model consisting of three lines 
of defence:

## First Line:

Led by the Group Chief Information Officer and comprises the Technology 
SBU and Information Security BU.

## Second Line:

Led by the Group Chief Risk Officer and comprises the Cyber 
and Information Security (CIS) BU, Operational Risk, Business 
Continuity and Resilience and Permanent Supervision. CIS is 
responsible for second-level control reviews across the Group’s 
cyber and technology risk environment, including third-party 
risks, and leads the Group’s offensive security activities.

## Third Line:

Independent audits are conducted by both 
MCB’s Internal Audit team and an external 
assurance provider, who assesses the 
resilience and effectiveness of our policies 
and systems annually.

MCB Group continuously enhances its cybersecurity controls and maintains robust incident response plans. 
 
The following plans are in place and are regularly tested and adjusted (including simulation testing):

Crisis 
Management 
Plan

Cyber Incident 
Response Plan

Disaster  
Recovery Plan

Business 
Continuity Plan

We ensure that our customers have clear access, control and protection over their personal data.

In addition to a secure login password, we provide additional security measures to limit unauthorised account access. These 
measures include, for example, a highly secure automatically generated one-time password (OTP). In addition, our security 
measures are underpinned by secure technology such as encryption, firewall mechanisms, temporary access denial and 
automatic timeouts.

We provide a detailed breakdown of the security mechanisms in place for customers on our website.

MCB does not commercially trade personal data, but does share it with agents, advisers, service providers, supervisory and 
public authorities for fraud prevention, regulatory or legal purposes and credit references.

We actively minimise our data collection and retention.

At MCB Ltd, we follow the principle of deleting (or anonymising) personal information after the stated periods defined in 
associated policies. Our approach aligns with the requirements of the DPA (2017), the European Union GDPR and the Bank 
of Mauritius. MCB adheres to the principle of data minimisation and ensures that only the personal data necessary for the 
stated purposes is collected, used and retained only as long as necessary.

## Employee training and awareness

We conduct regular employee training and awareness sessions on data privacy and security issues. 
 
Ongoing employee training is a key component of our data privacy culture. Training is delivered via the KnowBe4 platform, 
which offers a comprehensive number of courses on cybersecurity, compliance and fraud prevention, among others. These 
courses are regularly updated.

Training is mandatory for specific employees and business units, with mandatory courses covering data privacy, phishing 
and social engineering, general security awareness and protection of card data, among others. The CIS BU is responsible for 
ensuring that employees remain informed and compliant with current data privacy practices and regulations.

## Looking Ahead

In addition to the general security and awareness training delivered across the Group, policy 
awareness training was provided to overseas entities and local subsidiaries during FY26.  
Group-wide rollout is planned for FY27.

## Responsible product development and pricing review

We have policies and systems in place to help ensure that we apply responsible and fair practices in the 
development, review, pricing and implementation of products and services for our customers. 
 
These governance arrangements and processes support the alignment of product development and pricing decisions with 
the Bank’s strategy, customers’ needs, applicable legal and regulatory requirements and relevant risk management and 
control considerations.

Our Product Development Policy sets out the methodology and guidelines to develop new or review existing products or 
services to ensure these are responsive to our customers’ increasingly sophisticated and evolving needs.

## When developing new products and services, we follow the steps outlined below:

1.	
Concept development and validation: Colleagues from various business units, including the sustainability and 
compliance teams, collaborate to validate the design and development of new products and services or discuss 
proposed enhancements to existing products and services.

2.	
Detailed design: We conduct a ‘New Product Initiative Review’ early in the design process to ensure that any new 
product or service is checked for preliminary strategy alignment before product development begins.

3.	
Product development: Once we have developed a product or service concept, it is refined ahead of launch. Where 
relevant, customers may also test and evaluate the usability and functionality of a new product or service to support 
further refinement before launch.

4.	
Launch: Once validated, the product or service is launched.

5.	
Post-launch analysis: Where applicable, we conduct a two-phase, post-launch review: (i) 100 days and (ii) six months 
after the product launch. During this review, we assess the product lifecycle to ensure it is delivering the anticipated 
benefits for our customers and to identify any refinements or enhancements that might be necessary to ensure it 
remains effective.

The Bank regularly reviews its product and pricing governance arrangements to ensure their continued effectiveness 
and alignment with its strategic objectives, risk appetite, operational requirements, and applicable regulatory 
requirements.

## Debt collection

The Bank’s Credit Policy establishes a comprehensive framework governing its debt 
collection, restructuring and recovery activities. 
 
The Debt Restructuring and Recovery Management (DRRM) Business Unit is responsible for 
managing debt collection activities for both individual and non-individual customers. Delinquent 
customers are proactively contacted by either dedicated recovery team or relationship officers 
at an early stage to encourage the regularisation of arrears and to agree on a sustainable 
repayment arrangement. During all customer engagements, careful consideration is given to 
the customer’s financial circumstances, with the objective of identifying appropriate and viable 
solutions that support the restoration of their financial position.

Where arrears remain unresolved, or where agreed repayment arrangements are not 
honoured, accounts are escalated in accordance with the Bank’s established recovery 
framework. Depending on the specific circumstances of the case, this may include transfer to 
dedicated DRRM team for restructure, initiation of recovery proceedings, subject to predefined 
criteria and the requisite approval authorities.

Robust governance and oversight mechanisms are embedded within the arrears management 
process. Regular arrears review forums are conducted at both monthly and quarterly intervals 
and involve senior representatives from the Business and Risk functions. These forums provide 
a structured platform for monitoring portfolio performance, reviewing delinquency trends, 
assessing recovery strategies and determining appropriate corrective actions to mitigate credit 
risk and enhance portfolio quality.

Delinquent customers may, where appropriate, benefit from facility modifications or debt 
restructuring solutions tailored to their specific circumstances. Such restructurings may be 
undertaken on either a commercial basis or as part of a distressed debt management strategy. 
Restructuring solutions are assessed on a fair and consistent basis to ensure that all customers 
are treated objectively and in accordance with the Bank’s policies and risk appetite. The Bank 
remains committed to considering pragmatic and, where justified, innovative solutions aimed at 
helping customers restore their financial stability and meet their obligations sustainably.

As part of the restructuring and arrears management process, customers are encouraged 
to strengthen their financial discipline and improve their financial management practices. 
Depending on the circumstances, restructuring measures may include capital repayment 
moratorium, extension of loan tenor and maturity, repayment rescheduling, or the sculpting of 
repayment profiles to better align with the borrower’s projected cash flows.

We also apply a close monitoring approach to support customers’ turnaround efforts. This 
includes rigorous follow-up with clients on the implementation of their turnaround strategies 
and, where appropriate, the appointment of independent financial advisors to assist both the 
client and the Bank in monitoring financial and business performance.

Independent Business Reviews may also be requested, with the primary objective of preserving 
viable businesses and supporting sustainable recovery. In assessing and implementing such 
solutions, the Bank seeks to adopt an empathetic and collaborative approach, recognising 
the unique circumstances of each customer (especially for the individual segments). All 
restructuring solutions are subject to a thorough credit assessment and approval by the 
relevant delegated authorities in accordance with the Bank’s Credit Policy.

We seek to balance effective debt collection and recovery with a fair and empathetic customer 
approach. Employees involved in these activities receive ongoing on-the-job training, coaching 
and supervision to strengthen their technical expertise and customer engagement skills, 
while reinforcing adherence to the Bank’s policies, procedures and applicable regulatory 
requirements.

## Tax Responsibility

Our approach to tax emphasises ethical and responsible behaviour, and we continue to uphold a consistent tax 
framework grounded in transparency, compliance and effective risk management. 
 
We comply with all local legislations by continuously reinforcing existing controls, processes and reporting. The Group’s 
risk management and internal control framework ensures we adequately manage tax risks while ensuring compliance with 
established internal policies and procedures and relevant laws and regulations. We promptly handle any assessments raised 
by tax authorities with due escalation to the Board via the Audit Committee.

We keep up to date with any developments in tax legislation and integrate these changes into our tax strategy while ensuring 
tax optimisation. To support this, the relevant teams across our organisation regularly attend training sessions and seminars 
on tax and participate in various tax forums. We also seek assistance from independent local and international tax advisers 
to address complex or high-risk tax matters.

The Bank’s external auditors review our tax computations and tax disclosures, during which we provide supplementary 
information or clarification as required.

## Responding to evolving tax and regulatory requirements

In the Budget 2025-2026, the Mauritian government introduced a series of significant tax measures impacting the 
financial services sector, particularly banks.  
 
These measures included new contributions on chargeable income and resident/domestic profits, adjustments to levies 
payable by banks previously capped, the withdrawal of certain longstanding tax incentives and an expansion of VAT 
obligations related to foreign digital service providers. Other measures included a requirement to settle certain income taxes 
in foreign currency for transactions with non-residents and Global Business Companies12 (as far as banks are concerned), as 
well as the implementation of the OECD Pillar Two framework through a Qualified Domestic Minimum Top-Up Tax, which 
further aligns Mauritius with international tax standards.

## Pillar Two

In line with international tax reforms, Mauritius has introduced legislation implementing the OECD Pillar Two Global 
Minimum tax rules, effective for the year of assessment commencing 1 July 2025. These rules require large multinational 
groups with consolidated revenue of at least EUR 750 million to pay a minimum effective tax rate of 15%.

Following a preliminary assessment, the Group has determined that the combined effective tax rate for covered persons of 
MCB Group in Mauritius exceeds 15%. Accordingly, no top-up tax is expected to arise under the Pillar Two framework based 
on the current structure and prevailing tax rates.

The Group will continue to monitor developments and assess the potential implications of the Pillar Two income taxes 
legislation on its future financial performance.

11  Tax paid for MCB Ltd in FY26 includes corporate income tax, corporate climate responsibility levy, corporate social responsibility, VAT, fair share contribution and 
additional fair share contribution and special levy.
12  Global Business Companies (GBCs) are a form of business entity that are incorporated in Mauritius but specifically designed for international trade and investment.

## Respecting Human Rights

We are committed to respecting and protecting human rights across our operations and business relationships.  
Our approach considers potential human rights risks and impacts associated with our people, financing activities and supply 
chain, including forced and child labour, freedom of association and collective bargaining, fair remuneration, equality and 
non-discrimination, data privacy and health and safety. We are guided by the Ten Principles of the UN Global Compact and 
supported by relevant policies, due diligence and risk management processes. We continue to strengthen how human rights 
considerations are integrated into our activities and relationships with employees, customers, suppliers and the communities 
we serve.

## Responsible Sourcing and Supplier Conduct

Responsible sourcing and sustainable local procurement are business priorities. We are making steady progress 
refining our sourcing processes and building partnerships with suppliers who operate safely and responsibly.

## An overview of our approach

We encourage purchasing from local, Mauritius-based suppliers as far as possible, recognising the important role they play in 
promoting inclusive economic growth and local prosperity.

This includes goods and services related to facilities, maintenance, catering, cleaning, security, corporate functions and 
selected professional services, among others. In addition, Procurement is exploring a bulk oil initiative with the Catering 
Department, which could reduce the use of individual plastic bottles and cartons. Certain specialised services, such as 
technology solutions, consultancy and management of software licences, are sourced from foreign suppliers.

## Strengthening responsible procurement

We manage local procurement in accordance with established policies and processes:

•	
Our Procurement Policy sets out our procurement governance framework for the Bank, including outlining our 
commitment to supporting value creation, risk management and service quality.

•	
Our Supplier Code of Conduct sets out our expectations for suppliers in relation to responsible business conduct.  
The code is currently under review and will be amended to further strengthen sustainability-related expectations.

•	
We manage supplier-related risks and conduct due diligence using an internal framework that evaluates suppliers 
against key criteria, applicable regulatory requirements and standard banking practices.

This year, the Bank strengthened its responsible procurement practices by embedding sustainability, ethical and 
governance considerations into supplier onboarding, sourcing and approval.

•	
The Supplier Information Form, required during supplier onboarding, was updated to include sustainability-related 
questions for new suppliers. Questionnaires were also issued to existing suppliers to better understand their 
sustainability practices. In addition, conflict-of-interest declarations were introduced in the Supplier Information Form 
and the questionnaires issued to existing suppliers.

•	
We supplemented our existing supplier onboarding and Know Your Customer (KYC) processes by implementing the first 
phase of a risk-based Know Your Vendor (KYV) process to identify potential supplier risks, such as fraud, reputation or 
compliance concerns. Relevant findings are escalated for review, where required.

•	
We enhanced the Bank’s supplier governance processes by strengthening cross-functional collaboration to ensure 
sustainability-related requirements are incorporated into relevant contracts and requests for quotations or proposals. 
Representatives from SRE were appointed to the Bank’s Procurement Committee, further supporting the integration of 
sustainability considerations into procurement governance.

•	
To further strengthen objectivity in procurement decisions, Procurement was repositioned as an independent Centre of 
Excellence within the Finance SBU, reporting to the Group Head of Finance.

No material supplier-related risks or incidents requiring disclosure were identified during FY26.

Looking Ahead 
We will continue to strengthen our responsible procurement approach in the years ahead by 
improving the sustainability information available on our suppliers, deepening supplier engagement 
and progressively integrating sustainability considerations into sourcing and procurement decisions. 
This will support more informed assessment of supplier practices and help identify opportunities to 
strengthen local sourcing and supply chain resilience.

delivery and embed a customer-first culture.

MCB Group Limited | Sustainability Report 2026


---
*Page 204*


## Responsible and ethical business practices

## MCB Madagascar

Strengthened its personal data protection 
framework by introducing a specific clause governing 
the management and processing of personal data 
for new accounts. Developed with reference to 
Group standards and international good practice, 
the provision is intended to strengthen transparency 
and data protection. A phased rollout to existing 
customers is underway.

MCB Madagascar further formalised customer 
complaints management, introducing standardised 
processing procedures and strengthening the 
monitoring and management of customer requests. 
In addition, MCB Madagascar enhanced its approach 
to customer experience by establishing a dedicated 
Customer Experience department. The ad hoc 
Customer Experience Committee, comprising 
members of management, remained in place to 
provide cross-functional oversight of customer 
experience priorities and actions.

## MCB Maldives

Continued to strengthen its compliance 
and financial crime controls during FY26, 
including reviewing its Anti-Bribery and 
Corruption, AML and Economic and 
Financial Sanctions policies. Employees 
further participated in training and 
awareness, including refresher training on 
AML/CFT, suspicious transaction reporting 
and financial crime risk management 
as well as sessions relating to financial 
sanctions, fraud and scams, prudential 
regulations and customer due diligence. 
MCB Maldives strengthened cyber and 
information security awareness through 
training on AI, phishing, cybersecurity, 
general security and credit-card security.

203
MCB Group Limited | Sustainability Report 2026

Board oversight
IFRS S1 27(a) and IFRS S2 6(a)

for overseeing sustainability-related risks and 
opportunities, detailing their mandate, skills and

## Initiatives and Partnerships (MCB Ltd)

Below we provide a summary of the Initiatives and Partnerships sponsored by MCB in FY26. This summary includes 
a high-level overview of the key impacts and outcomes delivered during the year.

## Financial support: Rs 7 million

## Mauritian Wildlife Foundation

Our partnership with the Mauritian Wildlife Foundation supports the conservation of threatened 
endemic species and habitats across Mauritius and Rodrigues Island. This includes protection 
of four endemic species, biodiversity monitoring and education through the Islets Education 
programme and restoration activities at the Anse Quitor Nature Reserve.

## 666 Pink Pigeon population estimate

## 1,482 students attended educational talks

## 11 Round Island Boa visual population estimate

## Nearly 100 trainee teachers introduced to educational content12

## 12,559 visitors educated on island restoration

## 106 visitors to Anse-Quitor Nature Reserve

## Financial support: Rs 3 million

## Ebony Forest

Ebony Forest restores and conserves Mauritius’ native forests and wildlife through habitat 
restoration, conservation management and environmental education. MCB has supported the 
organisation since 2017. During FY26, our funding supported the Native Forest Restoration project, 
environmental awareness for school children and community groups and conservation efforts for 
threatened endemic species, including the Echo Parakeet and Mauritius Olive White-eye.

## 2 hectares sponsored

## 136 students reached through school visits

## 20,435 m2 of weeding and maintenance undertaken

## 10 hours of awareness sessions delivered

Conservatoire Botanique  
National de Brest

Financial support: EUR 30,000 
(2025 to 2027)

MCB has a long-term partnership with the Conservatoire Botanique National de Brest to support 
the conservation of two threatened endemic Mauritian plant species: Hyophorbe amaricaulis, 
classified as Critically Endangered, and Cylindrocline lorencei, classified as Extinct in the Wild. Our 
support contributes to long-term conservation and research aimed at improving propagation and, 
where possible, supporting the reintroduction of these species in Mauritius. This partnership is 
funded through MCB’s Your Card for Change initiative.

55 Cylindrocline lorencei plants transferred to Mauritius 
in July 2026

Survival and viability assessment ongoing following 
the exchange of expertise and rescue efforts between 
Conservatoire Botanique National de Brest and the National 
Parks and Conservation Service.

## Terres d’Agroécologie

Terres d’Agroécologie supports agro-ecology in Mauritius by providing training on establishing 
agro-ecological projects, conducting audits to promote the application of best-in-class agro-
ecological specifications and facilitating the marketing of agro-ecological products. We partnered 
with this organisation in 2022. In FY26, our funding supported training of agripreneurs, 
monitoring and certification activities and operational expenses.

## The Good Shop

The Good Shop is a Mauritian social enterprise that promotes the reuse and redistribution of 
second-hand goods while creating inclusive employment opportunities and supporting education. 
MCB partnered with The Good Shop in 2022. During FY26, our funding supported textile waste 
repurposing, inclusive vocational internships for women facing barriers to employment and 
improvements to the in-store experience.

## MCB Youth Championships

MCB has supported the MCB Youth Championships for more than 20 years. The annual event

platform for promising young athletes to excel, with some progressing to represent Mauritius at

## Samudra Art Prize

The Samudra Art Prize provides a platform for artists to explore sustainability issues through 
creative expression. In FY26, MCB supported the Young Artist category, contributing to event 
organisation and prizes. The 2026 theme, Delo, focused on water and encouraged young 
participants to raise awareness and inspire collective responsibility and action.

## National Spelling Bee Competition

The National Spelling Bee Competition is an annual event organised by the English-Speaking 
Union Mauritius with support from MCB Group since 2006. This initiative aims to promote English 
language skills among students and encourage them to expand their vocabulary, improve their 
spelling and learn the pronunciation and meaning of new words.

117 participating schools
412 participating students

## Science Quest

Science Quest is a project-based contest that encourages young scientists from across Mauritius 
to apply science- and technology-based creativity and innovation to tackle real-world challenges. 
This year, the content focused on desalination, bioplastics and promoting sustainable fuel 
consumption in Mauritius.

86 teams from 47 secondary schools participated

## Appendix 6: UNGC Principles

## FY26 Communication on Progress

MCB Ltd became a participant to the UNGC in 2008. This document is our annual Communication on Progress to 
stakeholders, with our progress and commitment to integrating the UNGC Ten Principles across MCB Ltd detailed in our 2026 
Sustainability Report. Relevant information can be found throughout this report, as detailed below.

## Human rights

Principle 1: Businesses should support and respect the protection of internationally proclaimed human rights

## Principle 2: Make sure that they are not complicit in human rights abuses

Our response
Read more

The Group ensures that human rights aspects are integrated 
throughout its value chain. We continuously engage with our 
key stakeholders at all levels, including employees, customers, 
suppliers and the communities we serve to mitigate any potential 
risk related to human rights.

•	
Risk management: page 59

•	
Financial Inclusion and Financial Health:  
page 85

•	
Responsible sourcing and supplier conduct: 
page 200

The application of the Equator Principles to project financing also 
contributes to upholding human rights. To ensure compliance 
with the Equator Principles, the Bank has established an 
Environmental and Social Risk Policy to guide its various teams 
and engage with customers, thereby being proactive in risk 
management.

•	
Mechanisms for managing employee 
complaints: page 148

•	
Respecting human rights 198

•	
Our Communities: page 155

## Labour

Principle 3: Businesses should uphold the freedom of association and the effective recognition of the right to collective 
bargaining

## Principle 4: The elimination of all forms of forced and compulsory labour

## Principle 5: The effective abolition of child labour

Principle 6: The elimination of discrimination in respect of employment and occupation

230
MCB Group Limited | Sustainability Report 2026

*Page 232*


## Appendices

Our response
Read more

We focus on fostering a positive workplace environment by 
promoting personal and professional growth and development. 
Child labour is prohibited in Mauritius and, in accordance with 
labour law, the Bank does not employ persons below 18 years. 
Similarly, MCB has zero tolerance to forced and compulsory 
labour.

•	
Shaping a strong, adaptive and inclusive 
culture: page 139

•	
Fair compensation and benefits: page 145

•	
Mechanisms for managing employee 
complaints: page 148

•	
Occupational health and safety: page 149

Employees of the Bank are free to join the MCB Employees’ 
Association, which is the trade union operating as per the national 
regulations (Worker’s Rights Act 2019). MCB has also established a 
Grievance Policy to help employees engage with their hierarchical 
manager before soliciting the assistance of the Employees 
Association to solve any unsatisfactory matter.

•	
Employee engagement: page 142

•	
Respecting human rights: page 198

60% of the Bank’s employees were covered by collective 
bargaining agreements (excluding overseas subsidiaries) for the 
period 1 July 2025 to 30 June 2026.

## Environment

Principle 7: Businesses should support a precautionary approach to environmental challenges

Principle 8: Undertake initiatives to promote greater environmental responsibility

Principle 9: Encourage the development and diffusion of environmentally friendly technologies

Our response
Read more

As a financial institution, MCB Group plays an important role in 
preserving natural capital wherever it operates by monitoring 
and managing its direct operational impacts and the indirect 
environmental impacts of its financing activities, integrating 
sustainability into products and services and encouraging clients 
and customers to adopt sustainable practices. We engage in 
long-term impact partnerships with local organisations across 
our home markets to address environmental challenges that go 
beyond our direct business activities.

•	
Sustainable finance: page 69

•	
Environmental Footprint 114

•	
Building sustainability awareness: page 121

•	
Initiatives and Partnerships: page 155

## Anti-corruption

Principle 10: Businesses should work against corruption in all its forms, including extortion and bribery

Our response
Read more

MCB has a zero-tolerance approach towards bribery and 
corruption.

•	
Responsible and ethical business practices 
page 178

We are committed to conducting business ethically, responsibly 
and in compliance with all applicable legislation, regulations, 
adopted industry codes and standards, and adhering to all 
internal policies and sound corporate governance principles. 
We foster shared responsibility for business ethics and integrity, 
ensuring a workplace built on trust, transparency and continuous 
improvement.
